## Prerequisites to teach first

- **Cell as the structural and functional unit; cell boundary, cytoplasm and nucleus** (outside): Teach what constitutes an individual cell before discussing cooperation, nuclei per muscle fibre, living plant cells, blood-cell drawings or neuron parts.
- **Plant and animal cells; plant cell wall, chloroplast and central vacuole** (outside): Explain the structures used to distinguish plant tissue cells and to interpret cytokinesis, photosynthesis and vacuolation.
- **Multicellular organisms, specialised cells and coordination of functions** (outside): Make division of labour and cooperation intelligible rather than memorised slogans.
- **Tissue, organ and organ-system levels** (book): Use the chapter's own organisation diagram before contrasting nerve cells with brain or bone tissue with a whole bone.
- **Classification trees: parent category, subtype and constituent** (outside): Teach that a subtype is not the same as a component and that branching arrows need not indicate time.
- **Diagram conventions: leader lines, brackets, flow arrows, enlargements and section views** (outside): Learners must distinguish labels from movement and compare lengthwise, cross-sectional and enlarged tissue views.
- **Somatic cells, reproductive germ-cell lineage, gametes and plant spores** (outside): Understand the occurrence and roles in the opening division table and recognise its life-cycle simplification.
- **Chromosomes, homologous pairs, sister chromatids, centromeres, haploid and diploid sets** (outside): Count chromosome sets independently of DNA quantity and distinguish homologues from chromatids during division and crossing over.
- **DNA replication in S phase and nuclear division versus cytokinesis** (outside): Explain daughter-cell number and explicitly diagnose the faulty replication row.
- **Prophase, metaphase, anaphase and telophase; meiosis I versus meiosis II** (outside): Give meaning to the phase counts and reductional/equational terminology without adding an unrelated full cell-cycle lesson.
- **Centrioles, centrosomes, asters, spindle microtubules, cell plate and cleavage furrow** (outside): Decode each plant–animal mitosis comparison and connect cell-wall structure to cytokinesis.
- **Diffusion, absorption, secretion and selective permeability** (outside): Explain epithelial transport and gland function and avoid confusing tissue transport with impermeability.
- **Cell shapes, single and multiple layers, exposed/apical and basal surfaces** (outside): Teach the actual axes used in epithelial naming and the position of basement membrane.
- **Cilia versus microvilli** (outside): Distinguish moving material along a surface from increasing absorptive surface area.
- **Glands and ducts; hollow-organ lumen; digestive, respiratory and urinary passages** (outside): Make epithelial location lists concrete, including kidney tubules, Bowman's capsule, urethra, ureter and bladder.
- **Surface abrasion, cell shedding, regeneration and keratin** (outside): Connect protective multilayering and keratinisation to wear at exposed surfaces.
- **Intracellular contents versus intercellular spaces and extracellular matrix** (outside): Distinguish gaps between cells from a cell's lumen or vacuole, interpret connective matrix and correct the parenchyma label.
- **Proteins, polysaccharides, glycoproteins and secretion of extracellular material** (outside): Explain the material basis of connective ground substance, collagen, elastin, reticulate fibres and contractile proteins.
- **Tension, tensile strength, flexibility, elasticity and recoil** (outside): Prevent strength, bendability and stretch-recovery from being treated as synonyms.
- **Bones, joints, tendons and ligaments** (outside): Explain skeletal-muscle movement and distinguish muscle–bone from bone–bone attachment.
- **Fat storage, energy reserves, heat loss and thermal insulation** (outside): Connect adipocyte appearance to storage, padding and the HPAS heat-conservation question.
- **Blood plasma, red cells, white cells, platelets, immune defence and clotting** (outside): Interpret blood as a fluid tissue and identify the components shown without importing detailed haematology.
- **Phagocytosis, antibody secretion, inflammatory cells and tissue-forming cells** (outside): Give concise meaning to macrophage, plasma cell, mast cell, fibroblast and mesenchymal-cell labels.
- **Muscle contraction, relaxation, cellular energy, fatigue and posture** (outside): Explain what muscular tissue does and qualify fatigue descriptions without requiring detailed molecular contraction mechanics.
- **Voluntary versus involuntary control and visible muscle striations** (outside): Keep control independent of banding and avoid misclassifying cardiac muscle.
- **Peristalsis, blood pressure, pupil size and heart pumping** (outside): Make the smooth- and cardiac-muscle function lists meaningful through concrete organ actions.
- **Stimulus, electrical nerve impulse, dendrite, cell body, axon and synapse** (outside): Establish the vocabulary needed to trace the supplied neuron diagram and answer its communication question.
- **Membrane electrical signals and neurotransmitter-mediated synaptic communication** (outside): Provide a standard-textbook mechanism for the book's otherwise diagram-only impulse question.
- **Root, shoot, stem, leaf base, node, internode, bark and girth** (outside): Teach the positional language used to distinguish meristems and their growth effects.
- **Mitosis as a source of new cells and differentiation as acquisition of specialised roles** (book): Use the book's division and differentiation sections before contrasting meristematic and permanent tissues.
- **Primary and secondary plant cell walls, cellulose, lignin, lumen and mature living/dead condition** (outside): Explain wall-based support and distinguish a remaining wall cavity from living cellular contents.
- **Photosynthesis, chlorophyll, chloroplasts and stored food** (outside): Connect chlorenchyma, storage tissue and phloem's transported material.
- **Air spaces, density, buoyancy and floating** (outside): Explain why aerenchyma aids buoyancy rather than merely memorising its name.
- **Dicot and monocot distinction; petiole, vascular bundle, pith, cortex, medullary rays, hypodermis, bundle sheath and pericycle** (outside): Decode the dense occurrence row in the simple-tissue comparison, using labelled sketches rather than an unrelated anatomy survey.
- **Root uptake, vertical/lateral transport, sieve plates, wall pits and source–sink movement** (outside): Explain constituent roles and avoid turning diagram arrows or summary-table claims into transport absolutes.
- **Plant epidermis, cuticle, stomatal pore, guard cells and cork-associated suberin** (outside): The final questions require these distinctions although the supplied exposition develops them only minimally.
- **MCQ evaluation: 'only', 'except', 'incorrect', and statements versus answer keys** (outside): Teach students to evaluate restrictive wording and not memorise a question's false statements as chapter facts.

## Concepts

### Tissue: cooperating cells and the plant–animal distinction · definition

book: Cells combine to perform the same function, forming a tissue; plant cells form plant tissues and animal cells form animal tissues. outside: Think of a tissue as a coordinated working group rather than a chance collection of adjacent cells; some tissues also contain substantial material outside their cells.

Items: p001-i2, p001-i3, p001-i4

### Division of labour and organisation · cause_effect

book: Tissue formation causes division of labour, decreases the workload on individual cells and leads to organisation. outside: Specialised groups share different jobs, so an individual cell need not carry out the complete range of functions needed by a multicellular body.

Items: p001-i5, p001-i6

### Levels of biological organisation · structure

book: The diagram orders the levels as Cell → Tissues → Organ → Organ system → Organisms, placing tissues between cells and organs and organ systems between organs and the organism. outside: These arrows represent levels of organisation in a multicellular organism, not successive stages through which one cell transforms.

Items: p001-i7, p001-i8, p001-i9, p001-i10, p001-i11, p001-i12

### Mitosis and meiosis: occurrence, frequency and products' roles · comparison

book: The table places mitosis in somatic cells and meiosis in germ cells, describes mitosis as more frequent and meiosis as less frequent, and associates their daughter cells with somatic organs and gametes respectively. outside: This is a simplified growth-versus-sexual-reproduction comparison, not a universal frequency rule; in plant life cycles meiosis produces spores, while gametes can subsequently arise through mitosis.

Items: p001-i13, p001-i14, p001-i15, p001-i19, p001-i20

### Mitosis and meiosis: divisions, phases and daughter-cell number · comparison

book: Mitosis has one nuclear division producing two daughter cells, whereas meiosis has two nuclear divisions producing four daughter cells. book: Mitosis has one prophase, metaphase, anaphase and telophase; meiosis has two of each, with five sub-phases in prophase I.

Items: p001-i16, p001-i17, p001-i21

### Chromosome sets: equational and reduction division · comparison

book: The table contrasts diploid mitotic daughters with haploid meiotic daughters, unchanged chromosome number with reduction to half, and 'Equation division' with reduction division. outside: The standard term is equational division: mitosis preserves the starting chromosome-set number, so its daughters are diploid when the parent is diploid; meiosis I is reductional and meiosis II is equational.

Items: p001-i18, p001-i22, p001-i25

### DNA replication versus chromosome-number change · comparison

book: Row 9 says chromosome number doubles at the beginning of mitosis, but not at the beginning of meiosis, and says doubling occurs after the first meiotic division. outside: This row is scientifically incorrect: DNA replicates before mitosis and before meiosis I, producing sister chromatids without ordinarily doubling chromosome number counted by centromeres, and there is no intervening DNA replication between meiosis I and II.

Items: p001-i23

### Crossing over in the mitosis–meiosis comparison · comparison

book: The table contrasts absence of crossing over in mitosis with its occurrence in meiosis. outside: In the standard comparison, meiotic crossing over exchanges segments between non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes during prophase I, helping explain why meiotic products can differ genetically.

Items: p001-i24

### Plant and animal mitosis · comparison

book: Table 7.3 contrasts plants with animals as follows: centrioles absent/present, asters absent/present, cytokinesis by cell plate/furrowing and cleavage, and mitosis mainly at meristems/in tissues throughout the body. outside: This is the standard higher-plant versus typical animal-cell comparison; the rigid plant cell wall helps explain cell-plate formation rather than cleavage furrowing.

Items: p001-i26, p001-i27, p001-i28, p001-i29, p001-i30, p001-i31

### Four main animal tissue groups · classification

book: The four main tissues in our body are epithelial, muscular, nervous/neural and connective: they provide covering or lining, movement, response to stimuli, and support, linking and cushioning respectively. book: The body montage presents parallel tissue examples and their bodily associations; its leader lines identify examples and locations rather than a sequence.

Items: p002-i1, p002-i2, p002-i26, p003-i1, p003-i2, p003-i3, p003-i4, p003-i5

### Epithelium: continuous sheets, barriers and basement membrane · structure

book: Epithelium covers most organs and cavities, lines the gastrointestinal tract and other hollow organs, and forms barriers separating body systems; its tightly packed cells form continuous sheets with little cementing material and almost no intercellular spaces. book: It is usually separated from underlying tissue by an extracellular fibrous basement membrane, shown as the supporting boundary beneath the epithelial block.

Items: p002-i3, p002-i37, p002-i38, p002-i44, p003-i2, p003-i6, p003-i7, p003-i8, p003-i9, p003-i10

### Reading epithelial shapes and layers · comparison

book: Fig. 6.9 compares squamous, stratified squamous, cuboidal and ciliated columnar drawings, while the later sketches also compare simple columnar, stratified cuboidal, pseudostratified columnar and transitional arrangements. outside: Read two separate features—cell shape and number of layers: simple means one layer, stratified means several, and stratified epithelia are named mainly by their superficial cells; these drawings are comparisons, not transformations.

Items: p003-i12, p003-i17, p004-i5

### Simple squamous epithelium · comparison

book: Simple squamous epithelium is a single thin layer of flat cells with irregular boundaries, illustrated as a sheet of flattened or tile-like cells. book: Its listed locations are blood-vessel lining, capillaries, lung air-sac lining and Bowman's capsule; its function is transport by diffusion where minimal protection is required.

Items: p002-i20, p002-i42, p003-i13, p003-i20, p004-i6

### Simple cuboidal epithelium · comparison

book: The diagrams show a single layer of roughly cube-shaped cells; the table describes short cylindrical cells, places them in kidney tubular lining and glandular ducts, and assigns absorption and secretion. book: Microvilli may occur in proximal convoluted tubules; outside: these are small surface projections that increase available surface area, unlike motile cilia.

Items: p002-i24, p002-i41, p003-i15, p003-i21, p004-i7

### Simple columnar epithelium · comparison

book: Simple columnar epithelium has one layer of tall, slender cells; the table lists digestive-tract and upper-respiratory-tract lining, says it is often ciliated, and gives protection, absorption, mucus secretion and movement in a specific direction as functions. outside: The row combines features of different columnar linings, so do not infer that each listed location has the same ciliation or arrangement; respiratory passages are also addressed by the pseudostratified row.

Items: p002-i22, p002-i40, p003-i22, p004-i8

### Ciliated columnar epithelium · structure

book: Ciliated columnar epithelium is a branch of epithelial tissue and is drawn as tall cells bearing hair-like projections on their upper free surface. outside: Cilia can move material along an epithelial surface, whereas microvilli chiefly enlarge surface area; the presence of cilia and the number of cell layers are separate identification features.

Items: p002-i23, p002-i39, p003-i16

### Stratified squamous epithelium and skin-surface illustration · comparison

book: Stratified squamous epithelium has several layers with flattened surface cells; the table lists mouth and vagina, protection, continuous shedding and regeneration, upward displacement and flattening of older cells, and a metabolically active columnar lower layer. book: The skin-surface block is separately drawn as thick and multilayered, while deeper cells in the comparison sketch are more rounded or cuboidal than the superficial cells.

Items: p002-i21, p002-i43, p003-i14, p003-i23, p004-i9

### Stratified cuboidal epithelium · comparison

book: Stratified cuboidal epithelium has a cuboidal upper layer, with deeper layers that may be cuboidal or other shapes, and is illustrated as stacked cube-like cells. book: Its listed locations are mammary, sweat and salivary glands, and its function is protection of gland ducts.

Items: p003-i24, p004-i10

### Stratified columnar epithelium · comparison

book: Stratified columnar epithelium has a layer of columnar cells over squamous, columnar or cuboidal epithelial cells. book: It occurs in the male urethra and lobar ducts of salivary glands and performs protection and secretion.

Items: p003-i25

### Pseudostratified columnar epithelium · comparison

book: Pseudostratified columnar epithelium contains cells of unequal height and nuclei at different levels, occurs in respiratory passages and ducts of many glands, and provides protection, secretion and movement of mucus. outside: Its apparent layering is not true stratification: the cells contact the basement membrane even though some do not reach the free surface.

Items: p004-i2, p004-i11

### Keratinised epithelium · comparison

book: The keratinised row gives the 'outer or apical layer of the cell' as location and describes mostly dead cells lacking nucleus and cytoplasm, with cytoplasm replaced by keratin to make the layer waterproof and protect against abrasion. outside: Interpret this as the superficial keratinised epithelial layer, not an anatomical location inside an individual cell; the skin epidermis provides a standard example.

Items: p004-i3

### Transitional epithelium or urothelium · comparison

book: Transitional epithelium is stratified, occurs in urinary bladder, urethra and ureter, and changes appearance with stretching: unstretched cells are described as cuboidal, whereas stretching compresses the tissue and makes cells appear irregular and squamous-shaped; the sketch shows large rounded superficial cells. book: It accommodates changing liquid volume and forms a tight-junction barrier against reabsorption of toxic substances; outside: the urethral location is region-specific rather than a description of its entire length.

Items: p004-i4, p004-i12

### Glandular epithelium and gland formation · process

book: Epithelial cells can specialise as gland cells that secrete substances onto an epithelial surface. book: Sometimes part of the epithelial sheet folds inward to form a multicellular gland, producing glandular epithelium.

Items: p002-i25, p003-i11

### Connective tissue: cells, matrix and binding functions · structure

book: Connective tissue connects and supports tissues and organs, cushions them, acts as binding and packing material, and prevents displacement during movement; relatively few, loosely spaced cells lie in an intercellular matrix that may be jelly-like, fluid, dense or rigid. book: Cells secrete a polysaccharide gel and fibres, and the diagram identifies collagen, elastic fibres, ground substance, scattered cells and a blood vessel, with collagen and ground substance bracketed as extracellular matrix; outside: more precisely, extracellular matrix comprises ground substance plus fibres rather than ground substance being synonymous with the entire matrix.

Items: p002-i5, p002-i45, p003-i5, p004-i14, p004-i15, p004-i16, p004-i17, p004-i18, p004-i19, p004-i20, p005-i1, p005-i2, p005-i7, p005-i10, p005-i11, p005-i12, p005-i13

### Collagen, elastic and reticulate fibres · comparison

book: Cell-secreted protein fibres account for connective-tissue strength, flexibility and elasticity: widespread collagen fibres are flexible with high tensile strength, whereas elastin fibres form stretchable networks that regain their original shape and size after force removal. book: Reticulate fibres contain collagen and glycoproteins, form thin delicate networks and join neighbouring tissues; illustrations distinguish thicker collagen strands from thin branching elastic or reticular fibres.

Items: p005-i3, p005-i4, p005-i5, p005-i6, p005-i9, p005-i14, p007-i29, p007-i32

### Connective-tissue classification and parallel examples · classification

book: The tree divides connective tissue into connective tissue proper—areolar, adipose and fibrous; supportive connective tissue—cartilage and bone; and fluid connective tissue—blood and lymph. book: Fig. 6.10 illustrates blood, compact bone, hyaline cartilage, areolar and adipose tissue as parallel examples, while the later table uses loose, dense, elastic, specialised and adipose headings rather than repeating exactly the same branching scheme.

Items: p002-i10, p002-i11, p002-i12, p007-i10, p007-i39, p008-i2

### Areolar and loose connective tissue: fibres and cell diversity · structure

book: Areolar tissue belongs to connective tissue proper and is drawn as a loose fibre network containing fibroblast, macrophage, mast cell and plasma cell, with collagen and reticular fibres; the earlier general connective illustration also labels a mesenchymal cell and macrophage. book: Loose connective tissue is listed in deep skin layers and in association with blood vessels, nerves and body organs, providing support and elasticity; outside: fibroblasts produce matrix components, macrophages engulf material, mast cells participate in inflammation, plasma cells secrete antibodies, and mesenchymal cells are relatively unspecialised connective-lineage cells.

Items: p002-i13, p005-i8, p005-i15, p007-i28, p007-i29, p007-i30, p007-i31, p007-i32, p007-i33, p007-i34, p008-i4

### Adipose tissue and adipocytes · comparison

book: Adipose tissue is connective tissue proper; its adipocytes are fat-storage cells with large fat droplets and nuclei near their edges, illustrated as rounded cells and soft-padding deposits. book: It occurs in deep skin layers, around the heart and kidneys and around joints, serving support, protection, heat conservation and energy supply.

Items: p002-i14, p002-i48, p002-i49, p005-i16, p007-i35, p007-i36, p007-i37, p007-i38, p008-i10

### Fibrous and dense connective tissue: tendons and ligaments · comparison

book: Fibrous tissue is a subtype of connective tissue proper; tendon is illustrated as a parallel fibre bundle, and dense connective tissue in tendons and ligaments packs structures together and provides great strength. outside: Tendons connect muscle to bone, whereas ligaments connect bone to bone at joints; tensile strength concerns resistance to pulling rather than rubber-like stretch.

Items: p002-i15, p002-i46, p008-i5

### Elastic connective tissue · comparison

book: Elastic connective tissue is listed in lungs, arteries, trachea and vocal cords, with elasticity as its function. outside: Relate this to the elastin-fibre property already taught—stretching followed by recoil—without treating the named organs as made entirely of elastic connective tissue.

Items: p008-i6

### Cartilage and chondrocytes · comparison

book: Cartilage is supportive connective tissue, also placed under specialised connective tissue in the table, and provides flexibility and support at long-bone ends, the nose tip and parts of the larynx and trachea. book: The hyaline-cartilage panel identifies chondrocytes as cartilage cells and hyaline matrix as their surrounding material.

Items: p002-i16, p007-i25, p007-i26, p007-i27, p008-i7

### Bone: support and compact-bone microstructure · structure

book: Bone is supportive/specialised connective tissue providing protection, support and muscle attachment; the body montage uses a long-bone-shaped example. book: Compact bone shows concentric units around Haversian canals containing blood vessels and nerve fibres, and a canaliculus contains a slender process of a bone cell or osteocyte.

Items: p002-i17, p002-i47, p007-i22, p007-i23, p007-i24, p008-i8

### Blood as connective tissue and identification of its components · classification

book: Blood is fluid connective tissue, also called vascular specialised connective tissue, located within blood vessels and responsible for oxygen/carbon-dioxide transport, immune response and clotting. book: The panel labels cytoplasm and nucleus, neutrophil or polymorphonuclear leucocyte with a lobed nucleus, granular eosinophil and basophil, large-nucleated lymphocyte, indented-nucleated monocyte, small platelets and a separate disc-like red blood corpuscle; outside: plasma is its fluid matrix, with red cells chiefly carrying respiratory gases, white cells contributing defence and platelets contributing clotting.

Items: p002-i18, p007-i11, p007-i12, p007-i13, p007-i14, p007-i15, p007-i16, p007-i17, p007-i18, p007-i19, p007-i20, p007-i21, p008-i9

### Lymph in the fluid connective-tissue branch · classification

book: Lymph is listed alongside blood under fluid connective tissue. outside: It is tissue fluid collected into lymphatic vessels, which makes the fluid classification understandable; the supplied page does not develop its composition or detailed functions.

Items: p002-i19

### Muscular tissue: contractile cells and three types · structure

book: Muscular tissue helps movement and consists of elongated, large muscle cells or muscle fibres containing contractile proteins and undergoing contraction and relaxation. book: Its three types are skeletal, smooth and cardiac; outside: here 'muscle fibre' means a muscle cell, unlike an extracellular collagen or elastic fibre in connective tissue.

Items: p002-i4, p002-i27, p002-i29, p003-i3, p005-i19, p005-i20, p005-i21, p005-i22, p005-i23

### Skeletal muscle: structure, names and functions · comparison

book: Skeletal muscle is attached to bones and has elongated, cylindrical, unbranched, parallel fibres with many eccentric nuclei and alternating dark/light striations; the sketches pair this structure with an arm and its bones. book: Its names include 'Skeleton muscle', striated and voluntary muscle; it supports body movement, heat production and posture, is voluntarily controlled and is described as easily fatigued; outside: fatigue varies with muscle-fibre type and activity rather than being an identical property of every skeletal muscle.

Items: p002-i7, p002-i30, p005-i26, p005-i27, p005-i28, p005-i29, p005-i30, p005-i31, p005-i32, p005-i33, p005-i34, p005-i35, p005-i36, p007-i4, p007-i5

### Smooth or visceral muscle · comparison

book: Smooth/visceral muscle, also called unstriated or involuntary muscle, has unbranched spindle-shaped fibres with tapering ends, one central nucleus and no striations; the initial tree misspells the label 'Non-straited', and illustrations pair smooth cells with intestine. book: Locations include stomach, intestine/G.I. tract, uterus, eye and blood vessels; functions include peristalsis, blood-pressure regulation, pupil-size change and hair erection, and the text says 'Do not fatigue'; outside: interpret this as resistance to fatigue in normal sustained activity, not an absolute inability to fatigue.

Items: p002-i8, p002-i31, p006-i1, p006-i3, p006-i4, p006-i5, p006-i6, p006-i7, p006-i8, p006-i9, p006-i10, p006-i11, p006-i12, p007-i8, p007-i9

### Cardiac muscle · comparison

book: Cardiac muscle lies in the heart wall and has cylindrical, branched, striated fibres with one central nucleus; its contraction and relaxation continuously pump and distribute blood, and its control is involuntary. book: The text describes it as not fatiguing and pairs its branching network with a heart; outside: this describes normal continuous physiological work, while one nucleus is the typical simplified description rather than an exceptionless rule.

Items: p002-i9, p002-i28, p006-i15, p006-i16, p006-i17, p006-i18, p006-i19, p006-i20, p006-i21, p006-i22, p006-i23, p006-i24, p007-i6, p007-i7

### Comparing skeletal, smooth and cardiac muscle · comparison

book: Compare location, function, appearance and control together: skeletal muscle is associated with skeleton, movement/heat/posture, parallel striated multinucleated fibres and voluntary control; cardiac muscle with heart, continuous pumping, striations and a central nucleus, and involuntary control; smooth muscle with visceral locations, peristalsis and regulatory movements, no striations and one central nucleus, and involuntary control. outside: Striation and voluntary control are independent criteria, so cardiac muscle is the key counterexample to 'striated means voluntary'.

Items: p007-i2, p007-i3, p007-i4, p007-i5, p007-i6, p007-i7, p007-i8, p007-i9

### Nervous or neural tissue and its bodily associations · structure

book: Nervous/neural tissue responds to stimuli, forms the fourth main animal-tissue branch and is illustrated by branched nerve cells associated with brain, spinal cord and a branching nerve near the shoulder/upper arm. outside: A neuron is a cell, whereas brain and spinal cord are organs and a nerve contains bundles of nerve fibres; these are related levels, not interchangeable names.

Items: p002-i6, p002-i32, p002-i33, p002-i34, p002-i35, p002-i36, p003-i4

### Neuron parts and connections between separate neurons · structure

book: The neuron diagram identifies cell bodies containing nuclei, short branched dendrites, a long axon, axon tips and synapses with the previous and next neurons. book: The incoming red process belongs to a previous neuron and the receiving upper-right cell is another neuron, so the connected drawing must not be read as a single continuous cell.

Items: p007-i41, p007-i42, p007-i43, p007-i44, p007-i45, p007-i46, p007-i47, p007-i49, p007-i50, p007-i51, p007-i52, p007-i53

### How neurons transmit impulses · process

book: The question asks how neurons send messages to other cells, and blue arrows show an electrical signal travelling along the lower-left neuron's axon toward its tips and the next neuron's dendrites, with synapses marked between cells. outside: A standard textbook answer is that an electrical impulse travels along the neuron and, at a typical chemical synapse, axon-terminal neurotransmitters cross a small gap and act on the next cell; that chemical mechanism is explanatory knowledge, not explicitly stated on these pages.

Items: p007-i40, p007-i41, p007-i42, p007-i43, p007-i48, p007-i49, p007-i52, p007-i53

### Plant-tissue classification tree · classification

book: Plant tissues divide into meristematic and permanent; meristematic tissue includes apical, lateral and intercalary meristems, while permanent tissue divides into simple and complex. book: Simple tissue includes parenchyma, collenchyma and sclerenchyma, with aerenchyma/chlorenchyma under parenchyma and fibres/sclereids under sclerenchyma; complex tissue includes xylem and phloem, whose opening constituent lists are cropped rather than complete.

Items: p001-i3, p001-i32, p001-i33, p008-i11, p008-i12

### Meristematic tissue: active, compactly packed dividing cells · structure

book: Meristematic tissue occurs in growing regions such as root and shoot, contains highly active cells focused on division, and has spherical, oval or rectangular cells, no intercellular spaces and absent vacuoles. outside: This contrasts actively dividing cells with mature specialised cells; the book's vacuole statement is a simplified description, as very small vacuoles may occur rather than a large mature central vacuole.

Items: p001-i34, p008-i13, p009-i3, p009-i4, p009-i5, p009-i6, p009-i7, p009-i8

### Apical meristem: tips and growth in length · cause_effect

book: Apical meristem occurs at both root and shoot tips and increases the length of root and shoot. book: The diagrams identify the terminal shoot apex and root-tip inset, with upward shoot and downward root arrows representing lengthwise growth rather than movement of the tissue itself.

Items: p001-i35, p008-i15, p008-i29, p008-i30, p009-i8, p009-i10, p009-i11, p009-i16, p009-i17, p009-i18, p009-i19, p009-i23

### Lateral meristem: beneath bark and growth in girth · cause_effect

book: Lateral meristem is described as present beneath bark and increases the girth of root or stem. book: Longitudinal strips along the stem sides and outward growth arrows distinguish this radial thickening from the length growth associated with apical regions.

Items: p001-i36, p008-i17, p008-i29, p008-i32, p009-i8, p009-i14, p009-i15, p009-i18, p009-i21, p009-i25

### Intercalary meristem: leaf bases and internodal regions · cause_effect

book: Intercalary meristem occurs at leaf bases or internodes and is illustrated as transverse bands near leaf attachment; one diagram label is clipped and misspelt, and the prose says it increases the length of organs like leaves and nodes. outside: A node is the leaf-attachment region and an internode the stem interval between nodes; intercalary growth is conventionally described as elongating leaf bases or internodes, which clarifies the imprecise reference to nodes.

Items: p001-i37, p008-i16, p008-i29, p008-i31, p009-i8, p009-i12, p009-i13, p009-i18, p009-i20, p009-i27

### Permanent tissue and differentiation · process

book: Permanent-tissue cells lose the ability to divide and take up specific roles; differentiation means acquiring permanent shape, size and function, and the overview lists a large central vacuole, thick wall, protection, support, storage and photosynthesis. outside: Treat these overview features as a simplified contrast with meristematic cells, not as universal properties: the chapter later shows thin-walled parenchyma and dead sclerenchyma lacking a vacuole.

Items: p001-i38, p008-i14, p009-i29, p009-i30, p009-i31, p009-i32, p009-i33

### Simple permanent tissue · definition

book: Simple permanent tissue contains one type of structurally and functionally similar cells and includes parenchyma, collenchyma and sclerenchyma. book: Its introductory line emphasises food storage and photosynthesis; outside: the subsequent types also provide support and protection, so that line should not be applied as the complete function list of each type.

Items: p001-i39, p008-i18, p010-i2, p010-i3

### Parenchyma: packing, storage and thin-walled cells · structure

book: Parenchyma occurs in stems, roots, leaves, flowers and fruits, has thin-walled round, polygonal or elongated cells with intercellular spaces, and stores food; the opening tree prints 'Paranchyma', whereas later pages use Parenchyma. book: The lengthwise and cross-sectional drawings show gaps between neighbouring cells; outside: the printed 'intracellular spaces' label is incorrect here, because the indicated gaps are intercellular, whereas intracellular means within a cell.

Items: p001-i40, p008-i20, p010-i6, p010-i7, p010-i8, p010-i10, p010-i13, p010-i14, p010-i15, p010-i16

### Chlorenchyma · classification

book: Parenchyma containing chlorophyll is called chlorenchyma and is shown as a subtype of parenchyma. outside: Chlorophyll in chloroplasts enables photosynthesis, linking this specialised name to the tissue's function rather than treating it as a separate main tissue branch.

Items: p008-i26, p010-i11

### Aerenchyma and buoyancy · cause_effect

book: Aerenchyma is classified under parenchyma and described as loosely packed tissue with intercellular spaces that provides buoyancy and helps plant parts to 'flow'. outside: The intended sense is float: prominent air spaces can lower tissue density and aid buoyancy, explaining the aquatic-plant association tested later.

Items: p008-i25, p010-i12

### Collenchyma: uneven thickening and flexible support · cause_effect

book: Collenchyma occurs in dicot stems and leaf stalks, has living elongated cells irregularly thickened at corners with very little intercellular space, and is illustrated in lengthwise and cross-sectional views with labelled cell walls. book: It gives mechanical strength, flexibility and photosynthesis, allowing bending of parts, with tendrils and climber stems as examples; outside: photosynthesis applies when the cells possess chloroplasts rather than being the defining feature of collenchyma.

Items: p001-i41, p008-i21, p010-i17, p010-i18, p010-i19, p010-i20, p010-i29, p010-i31, p010-i33, p010-i34, p010-i35, p010-i36

### Sclerenchyma: lignified support, fibres and sclereids · structure

book: Sclerenchyma provides strength and protection through dead, closely packed cells with lignin-thickened walls; occurrences include stems around vascular bundles, leaf veins, hard seed/nut coverings and coconut husk, and its forms are long tapering fibres and compact irregular stone cells or sclereids. book: The prose describes long narrow cells with no cavity because of excessive wall thickness, but the diagram explicitly labels an internal lumen in both forms; outside: the cavity is commonly narrow rather than necessarily absent, and the long-narrow description fits fibres rather than the full range of sclereid shapes.

Items: p001-i42, p001-i43, p001-i44, p008-i22, p008-i27, p008-i28, p010-i21, p010-i22, p010-i23, p010-i24, p010-i25, p010-i26, p010-i27, p011-i3, p011-i4, p011-i5, p011-i6, p011-i8, p011-i9, p011-i10, p011-i11, p011-i12, p011-i14, p011-i15

### Parenchyma–collenchyma–sclerenchyma comparison · comparison

book: Compare shape—parenchyma isodiametric oval/spherical/polygonal, collenchyma circular/oval/polyhedral, sclerenchyma variable fibres/sclereids; walls—thin cellulosic primary, unevenly thickened primary, lignified secondary over primary respectively; contents—parenchyma has abundant cytoplasm, nucleus and large vacuole, collenchyma has cytoplasm, nucleus and vacuoles, whereas mature sclerenchyma lacks these, and spaces are present/absent/absent in this table. book: Occurrence is given as soft packing regions including pith, cortex and medullary rays; dicot stems, petioles and beneath epidermis with absence in monocots and roots; and dicot hypodermis, bundle sheath, pericycle, seeds and fruit pulp respectively, while functions are storage/photosynthesis, tensile strength/support/photosynthesis, and protection against stress/strain with mechanical strength; outside: retain the table's wording but qualify its broad occurrence claims and reconcile collenchyma's 'absent' spaces here with 'very little' in the earlier prose.

Items: p013-i16, p013-i17, p013-i18, p013-i19, p013-i20, p013-i21, p013-i22, p013-i23, p013-i24, p013-i25, p013-i26, p013-i27, p013-i28, p013-i29, p013-i30, p013-i31, p013-i32, p013-i33

### Complex permanent tissue · definition

book: Complex permanent tissue contains more than one cell type whose cells coordinate for a common function, including transport of water, minerals and food materials. book: Its two types are xylem and phloem, shown as adjacent comparative drawings whose leader arrows identify components rather than transport direction.

Items: p001-i45, p008-i19, p011-i17, p011-i18, p011-i20, p011-i21, p011-i22

### Xylem constituents and division of work · structure

book: Xylem consists of tracheids, vessels or tracheae, xylem parenchyma and xylem fibres; thick-walled tubular tracheids and vessels, many described as dead at maturity, transport water and minerals vertically, parenchyma stores food and conducts water sideways, and fibres chiefly support. book: The structure drawings distinguish broad vessel elements from long narrow tracheids, label their pitted walls and show adjacent xylem parenchyma; outside: pits are wall regions facilitating lateral exchange, not evidence that the conduits are sealed against water movement.

Items: p001-i46, p001-i47, p001-i48, p001-i49, p008-i23, p011-i24, p011-i25, p011-i26, p011-i27, p011-i28, p011-i34, p011-i36, p011-i37, p011-i38, p011-i39, p011-i40, p011-i41, p011-i42, p011-i55, p011-i56, p011-i57, p011-i58, p011-i59

### Phloem constituents, sieve tubes and food transport · structure

book: Phloem transports food from leaves to other plant parts and is listed as comprising sieve cells, sieve tubes, companion cells, phloem fibres and phloem parenchyma; sieve tubes are tubular with perforated walls, and phloem cells except fibres are living. book: Its diagrams label sieve-tube elements and end/junction sieve plates, adjacent companion cells with a nucleus, phloem parenchyma, fibres and 'plasmo-desmata' connections; outside: mature sieve-tube elements remain living despite lacking their own nucleus, companion cells provide metabolic support, and plasmodesmata are cytoplasmic connections between neighbouring living cells.

Items: p001-i50, p001-i51, p001-i52, p001-i53, p008-i24, p011-i29, p011-i30, p011-i31, p011-i32, p011-i33, p011-i43, p011-i45, p011-i46, p011-i47, p011-i48, p011-i49, p011-i50, p011-i51, p011-i52, p011-i53, p011-i54, p012-i1, p012-i2, p012-i3, p012-i22

### Xylem–phloem comparison and limits of the summary table · comparison

book: The six rows contrast xylem/phloem as root-to-leaf water and mineral transport/leaf-to-growing-part food transport; dead/living cells; thick/thin walls; water-impermeable/food-permeable cells; lignin/cellulose composition; and mechanical strength/no mechanical strength. outside: These are overgeneralised summaries rather than tissue-wide absolutes: xylem parenchyma is living, phloem fibres are dead and supportive, cell walls are not entire cells made solely of one substance, lignification does not eliminate water exchange through pits, and phloem transport follows source–sink relationships rather than a fixed downward direction.

Items: p012-i4, p012-i5, p012-i6, p012-i7, p012-i8, p012-i9, p012-i10, p012-i11

### Stem vascular arrangement and interpreting transport arrows · structure

book: The illustrated stem has outer epidermis, cortex beneath it, central pith and a vascular region with inner xylem, cambium between, and outer phloem; a curved red arrow links the selected region to an enlarged view. book: In that enlargement, upward arrows occur in xylem and a phloem pathway has downward arrows with perforated partitions; outside: these black arrows show the depicted transport paths, not a universal claim that phloem transports food downward in every situation.

Items: p012-i12, p012-i13, p012-i14, p012-i15, p012-i16, p012-i17, p012-i18, p012-i19, p012-i20, p012-i21

### Plant surface protection and leaf gas exchange: question-required bridge · structure

book: The stem diagram identifies epidermis as the outer covering, and the later questions introduce wax-associated protection against water loss, stomata, guard cells and suberin without an accompanying explanatory section. outside: Epidermis can secrete a waxy cuticle that reduces water loss, stomatal pores permit gas exchange while guard cells regulate opening, and suberin is a wall-associated waterproofing substance especially associated with cork rather than the name of an epidermal tissue layer.

Items: p012-i13, p014-i6, p014-i8, p014-i10, p014-i11, p014-i12, p014-i13

### HPAS 2020 question: subcutaneous fat and heat loss · question

book: HPAS 2020 asks, 'Fat present below the skin surface in our body, acts as a barrier against:' with options a. Loss of heat from our body; b. Loss of essential body nutrients; c. Loss of salts from the body; d. Entry of harmful micro-organisms from the environments. outside (answer inference from the book's heat-conservation function, not a printed key): a is correct because subcutaneous fat provides thermal insulation; nutrient storage is a different role and the skin's external barrier should not be confused with the fat layer.

Items: p012-i23, p012-i24, p012-i25, p012-i26, p012-i27

### Practice question: meristem locations across pages 12–13 · question

book: Evaluate 1. Apical—present at root tips only; 2. Lateral—present beneath bark; 3. Intercalary—present at base of leaves or internodes; options continued on page 13 are a. 1 only, b. 1 and 2, c. 2 and 3, d. All are correct. outside (answer inference from the meristem descriptions, not a printed key): c is correct because statement 1 excludes shoot tips, whereas statements 2 and 3 match the book.

### Practice question: simple permanent tissue features · question

book: Evaluate 1. Parenchyma—large vacuole and stores food; 2. Sclerenchyma—in climber plants and provides mechanical strength; 3. Collenchyma—contains lignin; options are a. 1 only, b. 2 only, c. 1 and 3 only, d. All are incorrect. outside (interpretive answer, not a printed key): a is the likely intended answer because the book links climber examples to collenchyma and lignification to sclerenchyma, but statement 2 is poorly discriminating since climbers may also contain mechanically supportive sclerenchyma.

Xylem vessel. outside (answer inference from the constituent lists, not a printed key): A is correct because companion cells belong to phloem, whereas xylem includes tracheids, vessels and xylem parenchyma.

None. outside (answer inference from the wall comparison, not a printed key): b is correct; uneven corner thickening in collenchyma must not be confused with the lignified secondary wall of sclerenchyma.

book: The question asks for specialised parenchyma in aquatic plants providing buoyancy, with options a. Chlorenchyma, b. Sieve tube, c. Aerenchyma, d. Companion cells. outside (answer inference from the aerenchyma description, not a printed key): c is correct; chlorenchyma is associated with chlorophyll, while sieve tubes and companion cells belong to phloem.

They have features of both skeletal and visceral tissue; options continued on page 14 are a. 1 only, b. 1 and 3, c. 2 only, d. All are correct. outside (answer inference from the muscle comparison, not a printed key): c is correct because cardiac muscle is involuntary, sharing striations with skeletal muscle and involuntary control with visceral muscle.

book: The question asks which 'thickest' layer protects against water loss and secretes a waxy substance on its outer surface, with options a. Sclerenchyma, b. Epidermis, c. Parenchyma, d. Suberin. outside (standard-textbook inference, not a printed key): b is intended because epidermal secretion forms the waxy cuticle, but 'thickest' is defective wording since epidermis is commonly a single layer and a thick cuticle is a different feature.

book: 'Site of gaseous exchange on leaf is:' options a. Guard cells, b. Stomata, c. Aerenchyma, d. Chlorenchyma. outside (standard-textbook inference, not a printed key): b is correct because stomatal pores provide gas-exchange openings, while guard cells regulate those openings rather than being the pore itself.

### Practice question: muscle-to-bone connection · question

book: 'Muscle is connected to bone via:' options a. Ligament, b. Tendon, c. Cartilage, d. None. outside (standard-textbook inference, not a printed key): b is correct because tendons transmit muscle pull to bone, whereas ligaments connect bones to one another.

- Xylem–phloem comparison and limits of the summary table **depends_on** Phloem constituents, sieve tubes and food transport · book: The explicit phloem-fibre exception qualifies the summary table.

- Phloem constituents, sieve tubes and food transport **located_in** Stem vascular arrangement and interpreting transport arrows · book: Phloem is outer to cambium in the illustrated region.
