## Pages

### Page 1 · failed after 1 round(s)

A tissue is introduced as cells combining to perform the same function, enabling division of labour and organisation. The page shows the hierarchy from cell to organism, compares mitosis with meiosis and plant with animal mitosis, and classifies plant tissues into meristematic and permanent tissues with their visible subdivisions.

- **p001-i1** · heading · other · both
  - 10. Tissues and Its Types
- **p001-i2** · definition · definition · both · exam high
  - TISSUE: Cells combine to perform same function.
    - what: What is the defining idea of a tissue on this page? → Cells work together to perform the same function; the functional group is a tissue.
      - how: How should 'combine' be understood here? → It means that cells are organised and cooperate, not that they fuse into one cell. A tissue can contain one or more cell types contributing to a common function. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cell as the structural and functional unit of life
- **p001-i3** · list_point · classification · both · exam high
  - When plant cells combine, they form plant tissues.
    - compare: How are plant tissues distinguished from animal tissues here? → Plant tissues are formed from plant cells, whereas animal tissues are formed from animal cells.
      - how: How do plant cells form a functional tissue rather than merely a collection of cells? → Their arrangement and activities are coordinated for a shared role, such as growth, support or transport. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: plant cell; tissue
- **p001-i4** · list_point · classification · both · exam high
  - When animal cells combine, they form animal tissues.
    - compare: What distinguishes animal tissues from plant tissues in this introduction? → Animal tissues consist of animal cells working together, while plant tissues consist of plant cells working together.
      - how: How can specialised animal cells perform a shared tissue function? → Cells with coordinated properties contribute to a common task; for example, muscle cells contract to produce movement. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: animal cell; tissue
- **p001-i5** · list_point · cause_effect · both · exam high
  - Causes division of labour.
    - what: What consequence of tissue formation is stated? → Tissue formation causes division of labour.
      - how: How does division of labour operate in a multicellular organism? → Different groups of specialised cells perform different tasks, rather than each cell independently performing every function required by the organism. _(outside the book)_
        - why: Why is specialisation useful? → Cell structures can be suited to particular tasks, allowing tissues to perform those tasks efficiently while depending on other tissues for complementary functions. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: multicellular organism; cell specialisation
- **p001-i6** · list_point · cause_effect · both · exam medium
  - Decreases workload on individual cells, hence leads to organisation.
    - what: What relationship does the page state between tissue formation and organisation? → It states that tissue formation decreases the workload on individual cells and thereby leads to organisation.
      - how: How should the reduced workload be interpreted? → A cell becomes responsible for a narrower set of specialised tasks within a coordinated system. This does not imply that specialised cells are inactive or necessarily use less energy. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: division of labour; cell specialisation
- **p001-i7** · diagram · structure · both · exam high
  - Cell → Tissues → Organ → Organ system → Organisms. Right-pointing arrows connect successive levels of biological organisation.
    - what: What sequence of organisation is shown? → Cell, tissues, organ, organ system and organisms, in that order.
      - how: How should the arrows be read? → They indicate increasing levels of organisation: cooperating cells form tissues, tissues contribute to organs, and organs cooperate in organ systems within an organism. They do not mean that one cell simply changes into an entire organism. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cell; tissue; division of labour
- **p001-i8** · diagram_label · structure · both · exam high
  - Cell — first level; arrow leads to Tissues.
    - where: Where does Cell occur in the sequence? → It is the first level, before Tissues.
      - why: Why does the sequence begin with cells? → Cells are the living structural units whose coordinated activities build tissue-level organisation. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cell
- **p001-i9** · diagram_label · structure · both · exam high
  - Tissues — between Cell and Organ.
    - where: Which levels surround Tissues? → Cell precedes Tissues and Organ follows it.
      - how: How do tissues contribute to an organ? → Different tissues are arranged together and cooperate to perform the organ's functions. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: tissue; cell cooperation
- **p001-i10** · diagram_label · structure · both · exam high
  - Organ — between Tissues and Organ system.
    - where: Where is Organ placed? → After Tissues and before Organ system.
      - how: How is an organ different from a tissue? → An organ is an organised structure containing different tissues that work together, whereas a tissue is a cellular group performing a shared function. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: tissue
- **p001-i11** · diagram_label · structure · both · exam high
  - Organ system — between Organ and Organisms.
    - where: What follows Organ in the hierarchy? → Organ system, followed by Organisms.
      - how: How do organs form a functional system? → Several organs coordinate related activities, such as the organs involved in digestion cooperating to process food and absorb nutrients. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: organ; coordination of functions
- **p001-i12** · diagram_label · structure · both · exam high
  - Organisms — final level, after Organ system.
    - what: What is the final level shown? → Organisms.
      - how: How should the scope of this sequence be understood? → It describes increasing organisation in complex multicellular organisms. Unicellular organisms are complete organisms without this tissue–organ hierarchy. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: organ system; unicellular and multicellular organisms
- **p001-i13** · table · comparison · image
  - Two-column comparison headed Mitosis and Meiosis, with eleven numbered comparison rows.
  - prerequisites: cell division; chromosomes; nucleus
- **p001-i14** · table_row · comparison · image
  - Column 1: Mitosis | Column 2: Meiosis
- **p001-i15** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Mitosis: 1) It occurs in somatic cells. | Meiosis: 1) It occurs in germ cells.
    - compare: Which cells are associated with each division in the table? → Mitosis is associated with somatic cells and meiosis with germ cells.
      - why: Why are these cell categories associated with different divisions? → Somatic growth and repair usually require chromosome-set maintenance, whereas meiosis reduces chromosome sets in sexual life cycles. Germ-line cells can also multiply by mitosis before meiosis, so the table is not an exclusive rule. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: somatic cell; germ cell; chromosome set
- **p001-i16** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Mitosis: 2) Nucleus divides only once. | Meiosis: 2) Nucleus divides twice.
    - compare: How many nuclear divisions occur? → Mitosis has one nuclear division; meiosis has two.
      - how: How do the two meiotic divisions differ? → Meiosis I separates homologous chromosomes, and meiosis II separates sister chromatids. DNA replication precedes meiosis I but does not normally occur between the two divisions. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: nucleus; homologous chromosomes; sister chromatids
- **p001-i17** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Mitosis: 3) Two daughter cells are formed. | Meiosis: 3) Four daughter cells are formed.
    - compare: What daughter-cell numbers does the table give? → Two for mitosis and four for meiosis.
      - how: How do the division numbers account for these products? → One division generally yields two cells; two successive meiotic divisions generally yield four cellular products. In animal oogenesis these products are unequal, and they do not become four functional eggs. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: nuclear division; cytokinesis
- **p001-i18** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Mitosis: 4) Daughter cells are diploid. | Meiosis: 4) Daughter cells are haploid.
    - compare: How does the table describe daughter-cell ploidy? → It describes mitotic daughters as diploid and meiotic daughters as haploid.
      - why: Why does this comparison need a starting-cell qualification? → Mitosis generally preserves the starting ploidy: a diploid cell produces diploid daughters, while a haploid cell can produce haploid daughters. Meiosis from a diploid cell produces haploid nuclei by separating homologous chromosome sets. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: haploid; diploid; homologous chromosomes
- **p001-i19** · table_row · comparison · image · exam medium
  - Mitosis: 5) It occurs more frequently. | Meiosis: 5) It occurs less frequently.
    - compare: What relative frequency is stated? → Mitosis occurs more frequently and meiosis less frequently.
      - why: Why is mitosis commonly more frequent in a growing multicellular body? → Growth, replacement and repair involve repeated mitotic divisions in many tissues, whereas meiosis is restricted to particular reproductive cells and stages. This is a contextual comparison, not a numerical law for every organism. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: growth and repair; sexual reproduction
- **p001-i20** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Mitosis: 6) Daughter cells form somatic organs. | Meiosis: 6) Daughter cells form gametes.
    - compare: What roles does the table assign to daughter cells? → Mitotic daughters form somatic organs, while meiotic daughters form gametes.
      - how: How should the gamete statement be qualified in a chapter that includes plants? → In animals, meiosis is involved in producing gametes. In plants, meiosis typically produces spores; gametes subsequently develop in the haploid generation through mitotic divisions. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: gamete; somatic organs; plant life cycle
- **p001-i21** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Mitosis: 7) There is only one prophase, one metaphase one anaphase and one telophase. | Meiosis: 7) There are two of each phase and five sub-phases in prophase - 1.
    - compare: How are the phases distributed between mitosis and meiosis? → Mitosis has one prophase, metaphase, anaphase and telophase. Meiosis has these phases in each of its two divisions, with five sub-phases in prophase I.
      - how: How is prophase I subdivided, and why is it distinctive? → Its standard subdivisions are leptotene, zygotene, pachytene, diplotene and diakinesis. Homologous pairing and crossing over make this prophase more elaborate than mitotic prophase. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: prophase; metaphase; anaphase; telophase; homologous chromosomes
- **p001-i22** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Mitosis: 8) Number of chromosomes are not changed in the daughter cells. | Meiosis: 8) Number of chromosomes are reduced to half.
    - compare: How does chromosome number change in the two divisions? → Mitosis maintains chromosome number in daughter cells, whereas meiosis reduces it to half.
      - how: How is the halving achieved? → Homologous chromosomes separate in meiosis I, giving each daughter nucleus one chromosome from each homologous pair. Meiosis II separates sister chromatids without another reduction in chromosome-set number. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: chromosome number; homologous pair; sister chromatids
- **p001-i23** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Mitosis: 9) Chromosome number doubles at the beginning of each cell division. | Meiosis: 9) Chromosome number is not doubled. It doubles after the end of first meiotic division.
    - compare: What does this row claim, and should its wording be memorised as correct? → It claims chromosome-number doubling at the beginning of mitotic division and after meiosis I. These claims are scientifically incorrect as written and should not be memorised as valid differences. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How should replication and chromosome number actually be distinguished? → DNA replicates in the S phase before mitosis and before meiosis I. DNA amount doubles, but chromosome number counted by centromeres does not double at that replication step. There is normally no DNA replication between meiosis I and meiosis II. _(outside the book)_
        - why: Why is confusing chromosome number with DNA amount misleading? → A replicated chromosome has two sister chromatids yet is counted as one chromosome while their centromeres remain joined. Thus DNA amount and chromosome-set number describe different properties. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: DNA replication; S phase; centromere; sister chromatids; chromosome number
- **p001-i24** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Mitosis: 10) No crossing over in chromosomes. | Meiosis: 10) Crossing over occurs chromosomes.
    - compare: What standard crossing-over distinction does the table intend? → Crossing over is presented as absent in mitosis and present in meiosis.
      - how: How does meiotic crossing over occur and contribute to variation? → During prophase I, non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes exchange corresponding DNA segments, producing new combinations of alleles. This is a characteristic meiotic event; the table's mitosis statement is the standard introductory contrast. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: homologous chromosomes; non-sister chromatids; alleles
- **p001-i25** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Mitosis: 11) Equation division. | Meiosis: 11) Reduction division.
    - compare: Which division labels are printed? → Mitosis is labelled 'Equation division' and meiosis 'Reduction division'.
      - why: Why are these usually called equational and reductional division? → Mitosis is equational because chromosome-set number is maintained. Meiosis reduces chromosome-set number through meiosis I; meiosis II is itself equational. 'Equation division' is the book's wording for the standard term 'equational division'. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: chromosome-set number; meiosis I; meiosis II
- **p001-i26** · table · comparison · image
  - Table 7.3: Difference between mitosis in Plants and Animals. Columns: Plants; Animals. Four comparison rows cover centrioles, asters, cytokinesis and sites of occurrence.
  - prerequisites: mitosis; plant cell; animal cell
- **p001-i27** · table_row · comparison · image
  - Column 1: Plants | Column 2: Animals
- **p001-i28** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Plants: Centrioles are absent | Animals: Centrioles are present
    - compare: What centriole difference is shown? → The table states that centrioles are absent in plants and present in animals.
      - how: How can typical higher-plant cells divide without centrioles? → They organise spindle microtubules without a centriole-containing centrosome. The contrast describes typical higher-plant and animal cells rather than an exceptionless rule across all plant and animal groups. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: centriole; spindle microtubules
- **p001-i29** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Plants: Asters are not formed | Animals: Asters are formed
    - compare: How does aster formation differ in the table? → Asters are not formed in plant mitosis but are formed in animal mitosis.
      - why: Why is this linked with the centriole comparison? → Asters are star-like arrays of microtubules around centrosomes at the spindle poles in typical animal cells. Typical higher-plant mitotic spindles lack these conspicuous astral arrays. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: centrosome; microtubules; mitotic spindle
- **p001-i30** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Plants: Cell division involves formation of a cell plate | Animals: Cell division involves furrowing and cleavage of cytoplasm
    - compare: How does cytoplasmic division differ? → Plant cells form a cell plate, whereas animal cells divide by furrowing and cleavage of the cytoplasm.
      - how: How do these two mechanisms divide a cell? → In plant cells, vesicles contribute to a cell plate that grows outward and develops into the separating wall. In animal cells, a contractile ring draws the membrane inward as a cleavage furrow. _(outside the book)_
        - why: Why is the plant mechanism different? → The rigid cell wall limits inward pinching, so a new partition is constructed between daughter cells. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cytokinesis; cell wall; cell membrane; vesicles
- **p001-i31** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Plants: Occurs mainly at meristem | Animals: Occurs in tissues throughout the body
    - compare: Where is mitosis mainly located according to the table? → In plants it occurs mainly at meristem; in animals it occurs in tissues throughout the body.
      - why: Why is plant mitosis especially associated with meristems? → Meristems retain actively dividing cells responsible for growth. Animal growth and replacement involve dividing cells at many body sites, but the table does not mean that every animal cell divides. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: meristem; growth; cell replacement
- **p001-i32** · diagram · classification · image · exam high
  - Classification of Plant Tissue branches into Meristematic Tissue and Permanent Tissue. Meristematic Tissue branches into Apical Meristem, Lateral Meristem and Intercalary Meristem. Permanent Tissue branches into Simple and Complex. Simple connects to Paranchyma, Collenchyma and Sclerenchyma; Sclerenchyma branches into Fibres and Sclereids. Complex connects to Xylem and Phloem. Visible Xylem entries are Tracheids, Vessels (Tracheae) and Xylem parenchyma. Visible Phloem entries are Sieve tubes, Companion cells and Phloem parenchyma. The bottoms of the two constituent lists are cropped.
    - compare: What are the principal branches and subdivisions of plant tissue shown? → Plant tissue divides into meristematic and permanent tissue. Meristematic tissue has apical, lateral and intercalary meristems. Permanent tissue divides into simple and complex; simple includes the printed Paranchyma, Collenchyma and Sclerenchyma, while complex includes Xylem and Phloem.
      - how: How should the connectors and lower lists be read? → Connectors express membership, not a time sequence: fibres and sclereids belong to sclerenchyma, and the entries under xylem and phloem are their visible constituents. The cropped figure must not be treated as an exhaustive constituent list.
      - why: Why are simple and complex permanent tissues separated? → Simple tissues are mainly composed of one cell type, whereas complex tissues contain different cell types cooperating in a shared function. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: tissue; cell division; cell differentiation; classification tree
- **p001-i33** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Classification of Plant Tissue — root of the tree, connected to Meristematic Tissue and Permanent Tissue.
    - compare: Which two major plant-tissue categories originate from the root? → Meristematic Tissue and Permanent Tissue.
      - how: How does this first division organise the topic? → It distinguishes tissues with actively dividing cells from tissues whose cells have generally differentiated for particular functions. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: plant tissue; cell differentiation
- **p001-i34** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Meristematic Tissue — child of Classification of Plant Tissue; parent of Apical Meristem, Lateral Meristem and Intercalary Meristem.
    - compare: Which three meristematic categories are shown? → Apical, lateral and intercalary meristems.
      - why: Why are meristematic tissues linked with plant growth? → Their cells undergo repeated mitotic divisions, supplying new cells that can enlarge and differentiate. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: mitosis; plant growth
- **p001-i35** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Apical Meristem — child of Meristematic Tissue.
    - compare: Does apical meristem belong to meristematic or permanent tissue? → It belongs to meristematic tissue, alongside lateral and intercalary meristems.
      - how: How does its position explain its role? → Apical meristems occur at growing root and shoot tips and supply cells for primary growth, increasing length. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: meristem; root and shoot tips
- **p001-i36** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Lateral Meristem — child of Meristematic Tissue.
    - compare: Where does lateral meristem fit in the tree? → It is a meristematic tissue category, at the same classification level as apical and intercalary meristems.
      - how: How is lateral meristem associated with thickening? → Lateral meristems, such as vascular cambium and cork cambium, add cells that increase the girth of stems and roots where secondary growth occurs. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: meristem; stem and root girth
- **p001-i37** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Intercalary Meristem — child of Meristematic Tissue.
    - compare: Which major branch contains intercalary meristem? → Meristematic Tissue, not Permanent Tissue.
      - how: How does an intercalary meristem contribute to growth? → It lies between differentiated regions, commonly near leaf bases or internode bases in grasses, and supports elongation and regrowth. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: meristem; internode; leaf base
- **p001-i38** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Permanent Tissue — child of Classification of Plant Tissue; parent of Simple and Complex.
    - compare: What are the two subdivisions of permanent tissue? → Simple and Complex.
      - how: How does permanent tissue generally arise? → Cells produced by meristems differentiate into specialised forms and generally lose active division capacity. 'Permanent' does not mean that every such cell is dead or incapable of resuming division under suitable conditions. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: meristem; cell differentiation
- **p001-i39** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Simple — child of Permanent Tissue; connected to Paranchyma, Collenchyma and Sclerenchyma.
    - compare: Which tissues are placed under Simple rather than Complex? → Paranchyma, Collenchyma and Sclerenchyma are under Simple; Xylem and Phloem are under Complex.
      - why: Why are these called simple tissues? → Each is mainly composed of one cell type, unlike the cooperating different cell types in complex tissues. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: permanent tissue; cell type
- **p001-i40** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Paranchyma — printed label; child of Simple under Permanent Tissue.
    - compare: Where is the printed Paranchyma label classified? → Under Simple permanent tissue, alongside Collenchyma and Sclerenchyma.
      - how: How should this label be understood scientifically? → It denotes parenchyma, the standard spelling. Parenchyma commonly consists of living, relatively thin-walled cells that perform functions such as storage and, when chloroplasts are present, photosynthesis. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: simple permanent tissue; cell wall; chloroplast
- **p001-i41** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Collenchyma — child of Simple under Permanent Tissue.
    - compare: Is collenchyma a simple or complex tissue? → It is a simple permanent tissue.
      - how: How does collenchyma provide support without preventing bending? → Its living cells have unevenly thickened primary walls, commonly at corners, providing flexible support in growing plant parts. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: simple permanent tissue; primary cell wall
- **p001-i42** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Sclerenchyma — child of Simple; parent of Fibres and Sclereids.
    - compare: What subdivisions distinguish the sclerenchyma branch? → Sclerenchyma is a simple permanent tissue divided here into Fibres and Sclereids.
      - why: Why is sclerenchyma associated with rigid support? → Its cells typically develop thick, lignified secondary walls and are usually dead at maturity, leaving mechanically strong walls. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: simple permanent tissue; secondary cell wall; lignin
- **p001-i43** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Fibres — child of Sclerenchyma.
    - compare: How are fibres and sclereids related in the tree? → They are sibling subdivisions of sclerenchyma.
      - how: How does fibre shape suit a supporting role? → Sclerenchyma fibres are generally long, narrow, thick-walled cells, often with tapering ends; groups of them provide tensile strength. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: sclerenchyma; mechanical support
- **p001-i44** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Sclereids — child of Sclerenchyma.
    - compare: Which tissue contains sclereids, and what is their sibling category? → Sclerenchyma contains sclereids and fibres.
      - how: How do sclereids generally differ in form from fibres? → Sclereids are commonly shorter and more irregularly shaped than fibres, with thick lignified walls that contribute hardness, as in gritty pear flesh and hard seed coverings. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: sclerenchyma; lignified wall; fibre
- **p001-i45** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Complex — child of Permanent Tissue; connected to Xylem and Phloem.
    - compare: Which tissues belong to Complex rather than Simple? → Xylem and Phloem belong to Complex permanent tissue.
      - why: Why are xylem and phloem called complex tissues? → Each combines different cell types with complementary transport, support and storage roles. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: permanent tissue; different cell types
- **p001-i46** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Xylem — child of Complex; visible entries below it are Tracheids, Vessels (Tracheae) and Xylem parenchyma.
    - compare: Which visible constituents belong to xylem rather than phloem? → Tracheids, Vessels (Tracheae) and Xylem parenchyma are shown under Xylem.
      - how: How do different xylem cells cooperate? → Tracheids and vessels conduct water and dissolved minerals, while living xylem parenchyma contributes storage and lateral transport. The visible cropped list is not a complete inventory of xylem cell types. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: complex tissue; water and mineral transport
- **p001-i47** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Tracheids — constituent listed under Xylem.
    - compare: Are tracheids placed under xylem or phloem? → Under Xylem.
      - how: How can a mature tracheid conduct water? → A mature tracheid is an elongated, dead, lignified cell with a hollow interior; water passes between neighbouring cells through pits. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: xylem; lignified cell wall; pits
- **p001-i48** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Vessels (Tracheae) — constituent listed under Xylem.
    - compare: Where are Vessels (Tracheae) classified? → As constituents of Xylem, alongside the visible Tracheids and Xylem parenchyma entries.
      - how: How is a vessel organised for transport? → Vessel elements join end to end, with perforated end walls forming a water-conducting tube; the conducting elements are dead at maturity. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: xylem; vessel element; water transport
- **p001-i49** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Xylem parenchyma — constituent listed under Xylem.
    - compare: How is xylem parenchyma placed relative to the simple-tissue parenchyma label? → The diagram separately lists Xylem parenchyma as a constituent of the complex tissue Xylem, while the printed Paranchyma label is under Simple.
      - why: Why is parenchyma present within a complex tissue? → Complex tissue combines cell types; its parenchyma cells remain living and contribute storage and lateral movement of substances alongside other specialised elements. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: parenchyma; complex tissue; xylem
- **p001-i50** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Phloem — child of Complex; visible entries below it are Sieve tubes, Companion cells and Phloem parenchyma.
    - compare: Which visible constituents belong to phloem? → Sieve tubes, Companion cells and Phloem parenchyma.
      - how: How do these different cell types support phloem function? → Sieve tubes transport organic nutrients, companion cells support their functioning, and phloem parenchyma contributes storage and local movement of substances. The cropped list should not be assumed exhaustive. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: complex tissue; transport of sugars
- **p001-i51** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Sieve tubes — constituent listed under Phloem.
    - compare: Are sieve tubes xylem or phloem constituents? → They are Phloem constituents.
      - how: How are sieve tubes suited to moving food materials? → Living sieve-tube elements join end to end with sieve plates that allow movement of phloem sap. Mature elements lack a nucleus and depend on associated companion cells for important metabolic support. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: phloem; sieve plate; nucleus; organic nutrients
- **p001-i52** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Companion cells — constituent listed under Phloem.
    - compare: Which complex tissue contains the companion cells shown? → Phloem, not Xylem.
      - how: How do companion cells assist sieve-tube elements? → They are living, nucleated cells connected to sieve-tube elements and provide metabolic support, including participation in sugar loading and unloading. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: phloem; sieve-tube element; nucleus
- **p001-i53** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Phloem parenchyma — constituent listed under Phloem.
    - compare: How are the two named vascular parenchyma entries placed? → Xylem parenchyma is under Xylem, while Phloem parenchyma is under Phloem.
      - how: How does phloem parenchyma contribute to its tissue? → Its living cells store substances and assist local movement of food materials, complementing the conducting and supporting roles of other phloem elements. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: parenchyma; phloem; storage and transport

### Page 2 · passed after 0 round(s)

Animal tissues are classified into epithelial, muscular, connective and nervous/neural tissues. The tree lists six epithelial forms, three muscle types and three connective-tissue subdivisions with their examples. A second illustration shows muscle cells, epithelial arrangements, nervous structures and connective-tissue examples alongside the human body.

- **p002-i1** · diagram · classification · image
  - Animal Tissues branches into Epithelial Tissue, Muscular Tissue, Connective Tissue, and Nervous / Neural Tissue. Epithelial Tissue branches into Simple squamous epithelium, Stratified squamous epithelium, Columnar epithelium, Ciliated columnar epithelium, Cuboidal epithelium, and Glandular epithelium. Muscular Tissue branches into Striated muscle, Non-straited muscle, and Cardiac muscle. Connective Tissue branches into Connective tissue proper, Supportive connective tissue, and Fluid connective tissue. Connective tissue proper branches into Areolar tissue, Adipose tissue, and Fibrous tissue. Supportive connective tissue branches into Cartilage and Bone. Fluid connective tissue branches into Blood and Lymph. Nervous / Neural Tissue has no further branch shown. Arrows lead from each parent to its subtypes.
  - prerequisites: cells forming tissues; parent categories and subcategories in a classification tree
- **p002-i2** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Animal Tissues — root of the tree, branching into Epithelial Tissue, Muscular Tissue, Connective Tissue, and Nervous / Neural Tissue.
    - compare: Which categories occupy the same first level under Animal Tissues? → Epithelial, muscular, connective, and nervous/neural tissues are the four parallel main categories shown.
      - how: How should a learner read the levels of this tree? → Read from the general parent to increasingly specific subgroups. For example, blood belongs under fluid connective tissue, which belongs under connective tissue; blood is not a fifth main animal-tissue group.
  - prerequisites: meaning of a tissue; hierarchical classification
- **p002-i3** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Epithelial Tissue — main branch of Animal Tissues; connects downward to Simple squamous epithelium, Stratified squamous epithelium, Columnar epithelium, Ciliated columnar epithelium, Cuboidal epithelium, and Glandular epithelium.
    - compare: Which forms are grouped under Epithelial Tissue? → The six displayed forms are simple squamous, stratified squamous, columnar, ciliated columnar, cuboidal, and glandular epithelium.
      - why: Why do these epithelial names refer to different kinds of features? → Epithelia can be described by cell shape, number of layers, surface specialisations or secretory function. Thus squamous and cuboidal refer to shape, stratified to layers, ciliated to a surface feature, and glandular to secretion. The figure is a teaching list rather than a set of mutually exclusive criteria. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cell shape; cell layers; tissues
- **p002-i4** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Muscular Tissue — main branch of Animal Tissues; branches into Striated muscle, Non-straited muscle, and Cardiac muscle.
    - compare: What three muscle categories does the tree distinguish? → It distinguishes Striated muscle, the printed Non-straited muscle category, and Cardiac muscle.
      - how: How should the separate cardiac branch be understood when cardiac muscle also has striations? → This common three-part school classification uses striated muscle for skeletal muscle, non-striated for smooth muscle, and a separate cardiac category for heart muscle. Cardiac muscle itself is striated but differs from skeletal muscle in structure and control. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: muscle contraction; meaning of a subtype
- **p002-i5** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Connective Tissue — main branch of Animal Tissues; branches into Connective tissue proper, Supportive connective tissue, and Fluid connective tissue.
    - compare: How is connective tissue subdivided in this tree? → It is divided into connective tissue proper, supportive connective tissue and fluid connective tissue.
      - why: Why can both bone and blood belong to connective tissue despite looking very different? → Connective tissues have cells associated with an extracellular matrix. The matrix can be rigid in bone or fluid in blood, allowing different connective tissues to support, bind or transport. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cells; extracellular matrix
- **p002-i6** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Nervous / Neural Tissue — fourth main branch of Animal Tissues; no sub-branches are shown.
    - compare: Are nervous tissue and neural tissue separate main categories here? → No. The slash presents Nervous and Neural as alternative names for the same main tissue category.
      - how: How does nervous tissue contribute to coordination? → Neurons receive and transmit signals, allowing information about conditions or stimuli to reach other cells and helping coordinate responses. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cells; signals between cells
- **p002-i7** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Striated muscle — a branch of Muscular Tissue.
    - compare: What is the structural distinction between striated and non-striated muscle? → Striated muscle has visible transverse light and dark bands; non-striated muscle lacks this banded appearance. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why do striations appear? → Contractile filaments are arranged in repeating units called sarcomeres, producing a regular banding pattern. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: muscle cells; contractile filaments
- **p002-i8** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Non-straited muscle — printed label for the middle branch of Muscular Tissue.
    - compare: Which standard muscle category does the printed 'Non-straited muscle' label represent? → It represents non-striated, or smooth, muscle, contrasting with the striated branch. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why is smooth muscle called non-striated? → Its contractile filaments are not arranged into the repeating sarcomeres that produce visible transverse bands in skeletal and cardiac muscle. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: muscle cells; striations
- **p002-i9** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Cardiac muscle — a branch of Muscular Tissue.
    - compare: How does cardiac muscle compare with skeletal muscle? → Both are striated, but cardiac muscle forms the contractile tissue of the heart and acts involuntarily; skeletal muscle generally produces voluntary body movements. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How is cardiac muscle suited to pumping? → Its interconnected muscle cells contract in a coordinated way, generating the force needed to move blood through the circulation. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: heart pumping blood; muscle contraction
- **p002-i10** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Connective tissue proper — child of Connective Tissue; parent of Areolar tissue, Adipose tissue, and Fibrous tissue.
    - compare: Which examples belong to connective tissue proper rather than the supportive or fluid subdivisions in this chart? → Areolar, adipose and fibrous tissues belong to connective tissue proper.
      - how: How can these tissues have different functions within the same broad subdivision? → Their proportions of cells, fibres and ground substance differ. Loose packing, fat storage and strong fibrous binding are therefore possible within this connective-tissue grouping. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: extracellular matrix; connective tissue
- **p002-i11** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Supportive connective tissue — child of Connective Tissue; branches into Cartilage and Bone.
    - compare: Which two tissues are paired under supportive connective tissue? → Cartilage and bone are the two displayed examples.
      - why: Why are they classed as supportive? → Their substantial extracellular matrices provide structural support. Cartilage offers relatively flexible support, whereas mineralised bone provides greater rigidity. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: extracellular matrix; structural support
- **p002-i12** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Fluid connective tissue — child of Connective Tissue; branches into Blood and Lymph.
    - compare: Which examples are placed under fluid rather than supportive connective tissue? → Blood and lymph are fluid connective tissues; cartilage and bone are in the supportive subdivision.
      - why: Why does a fluid medium suit these tissues? → A fluid medium permits movement of dissolved substances and suspended cells, supporting transport and exchange rather than rigid support. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: fluid; extracellular matrix; transport in the body
- **p002-i13** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Areolar tissue — a subtype of Connective tissue proper.
    - compare: Where does areolar tissue belong relative to cartilage? → Areolar tissue belongs to connective tissue proper, while cartilage belongs to supportive connective tissue.
      - how: How does areolar tissue serve as packing tissue? → Its loosely arranged fibres and cells in a soft ground substance allow it to occupy spaces, support small vessels and help bind adjacent tissues. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: connective-tissue matrix; fibres
- **p002-i14** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Adipose tissue — a subtype of Connective tissue proper.
    - compare: Is adipose tissue placed with connective tissue proper or fluid connective tissue? → It is placed with connective tissue proper, alongside areolar and fibrous tissues.
      - how: How do fat-storing cells make adipose tissue useful? → Stored lipid provides an energy reserve, while deposits of these cells can provide cushioning and reduce heat loss. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: fat as an energy store; cells forming tissues
- **p002-i15** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Fibrous tissue — a subtype of Connective tissue proper.
    - compare: Which subdivision contains fibrous tissue rather than bone? → Fibrous tissue is under connective tissue proper; bone is under supportive connective tissue.
      - why: Why are fibres important for strong connections? → Abundant collagen fibres resist pulling forces, helping fibrous connective tissues bind structures and transmit tension. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: connective-tissue fibres; pulling force
- **p002-i16** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Cartilage — a subtype of Supportive connective tissue.
    - compare: How does cartilage compare with bone as a supporting tissue? → Both provide support, but cartilage has a relatively flexible matrix, whereas bone has a harder mineralised matrix. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How can flexible support be useful? → A resilient matrix can maintain shape while allowing deformation and can cushion forces at suitable locations, such as joint surfaces. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: extracellular matrix; flexibility and rigidity
- **p002-i17** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Bone — a subtype of Supportive connective tissue.
    - compare: Where does bone belong relative to blood in this classification? → Both are connective tissues, but bone is supportive and blood is fluid connective tissue.
      - why: Why is bone suited to rigid support? → Mineral salts deposited in its collagen-containing matrix give it hardness and resistance to compression. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: extracellular matrix; mineralisation
- **p002-i18** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Blood — a subtype of Fluid connective tissue.
    - compare: Which tissue shares blood's immediate parent in the tree? → Lymph shares the parent Fluid connective tissue with blood.
      - why: Why is blood considered a tissue although it flows? → Its cells and cell fragments function together in a fluid extracellular matrix, plasma. A tissue need not form a solid sheet. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cells forming tissues; plasma; extracellular matrix
- **p002-i19** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Lymph — a subtype of Fluid connective tissue.
    - compare: How is lymph classified in relation to blood? → Lymph and blood are both placed under Fluid connective tissue.
      - how: How does lymph help link body tissues with circulation? → Fluid entering lymphatic vessels from tissue spaces is carried as lymph and eventually returned to the bloodstream. Lymph also carries immune cells. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: tissue fluid; blood circulation; lymphatic vessels
- **p002-i20** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Simple squamous epithelium — a subtype of Epithelial Tissue.
    - compare: How does simple squamous differ from stratified squamous epithelium? → Simple squamous epithelium has one layer of flattened cells; stratified squamous epithelium has several layers, with flattened cells at the exposed surface. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why can a single thin layer favour exchange? → A thin barrier shortens the distance through which substances must diffuse. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cell layers; flattened cells; diffusion
- **p002-i21** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Stratified squamous epithelium — a subtype of Epithelial Tissue.
    - compare: What distinguishes stratified squamous from simple squamous epithelium? → Stratified squamous epithelium has multiple cell layers rather than one; the surface cells are flattened. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why are multiple layers useful at surfaces exposed to wear? → Superficial cells can be lost through abrasion while deeper layers continue to maintain a protective barrier. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cell layers; abrasion; epithelial barrier
- **p002-i22** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Columnar epithelium — a subtype of Epithelial Tissue.
    - compare: How do columnar cells differ in shape from squamous cells? → Columnar cells are relatively tall and column-like, whereas squamous cells are flattened. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How can columnar cells contribute to lining functions? → Their cellular machinery and specialised surfaces can support absorption or secretion, depending on the organ. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cell shape; absorption; secretion
- **p002-i23** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Ciliated columnar epithelium — a subtype of Epithelial Tissue.
    - compare: What additional feature distinguishes ciliated columnar epithelium from non-ciliated columnar epithelium? → Ciliated columnar cells bear motile hair-like cilia on their exposed surface. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How do cilia help a lining perform its function? → Their coordinated beating moves material along the epithelial surface, such as mucus in parts of the respiratory tract. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: columnar cell shape; cilia; epithelial surface
- **p002-i24** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Cuboidal epithelium — a subtype of Epithelial Tissue.
    - compare: How does cuboidal cell shape differ from columnar and squamous shapes? → Cuboidal cells have roughly similar height and width; columnar cells are taller, while squamous cells are thin and flattened. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How can cuboidal epithelium contribute to a tubule or duct? → It forms a lining whose cells can carry out absorption or secretion, as in kidney tubules and many small gland ducts. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: cell shape; tubules and ducts; absorption and secretion
- **p002-i25** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Glandular epithelium — a subtype of Epithelial Tissue.
    - compare: How does the name glandular differ from names such as cuboidal or squamous? → Glandular emphasises secretory specialisation, whereas cuboidal and squamous describe cell shape. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How does glandular epithelium perform secretion? → Its cells synthesise and release products, such as mucus or enzymes, either as individual secretory cells or as organised glands. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: secretion; glands; epithelial cells

Leader lines associate the tissue examples with locations or structures on the body; they are not a sequential process.

- **p002-i27** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high

      - how: How does this terminology connect with the upper tree? → Skeletal muscle corresponds to the tree's striated branch, smooth muscle to its non-striated branch, and cardiac muscle retains the same name. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: upper muscle classification; muscle cells
- **p002-i28** · diagram_label · structure · image · exam high
