- **p013-i16** · table · comparison · image
  - Comparison of Parenchyma, Collenchyma and Sclerenchyma. Columns: Features; Parenchyma; Collenchyma; Sclerenchyma. Illustrated header followed by eight feature rows: Cell shape, Cell wall, Cytoplasm, Nucleus, Vacuoles, Intercellular spaces, Occurrence and Functions.
- **p013-i17** · table_row · other · image
  - Features | Parenchyma | Collenchyma | Sclerenchyma
- **p013-i18** · diagram · comparison · image · exam high
  - Three side-by-side tissue drawings under Parenchyma, Collenchyma and Sclerenchyma. Parenchyma shows relatively thin-walled cells with spaces between them; collenchyma shows closely arranged cells with unevenly thickened walls; sclerenchyma shows thick-walled cells with comparatively small internal spaces. Leader lines identify thin primary cell wall, irregularly thickened primary cell wall, thick secondary cell wall and primary cell wall. This is a structural comparison, not a sequence or flow.
    - compare: What structural contrast do the three drawings emphasise? → They contrast thin primary walls in parenchyma, irregular primary-wall thickening in collenchyma and thick secondary walls in sclerenchyma.
      - how: How does wall structure help explain functional differences? → Thin-walled living cells suit storage and metabolism; uneven primary-wall thickening provides flexible support; thick lignified secondary walls provide stronger, more rigid reinforcement. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Plant cell wall; Primary and secondary walls; Cell lumen; Intercellular spaces
- **p013-i19** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Parenchyma — names the first tissue drawing.
    - compare: Which illustrated features distinguish parenchyma from the other two tissues? → Its cells have thin walls and visible spaces between neighbouring cells.
      - why: Why should spaces between cells not be confused with vacuoles? → An intercellular space lies outside neighbouring cells, whereas a vacuole is a compartment inside a living cell. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Cell boundary; Vacuole; Intercellular space
- **p013-i20** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Collenchyma — names the middle tissue drawing.
    - compare: What distinguishes the collenchyma drawing? → It depicts closely arranged cells with unevenly thickened primary walls.
      - how: How can the tissue provide support without the rigidity associated with sclerenchyma? → Its thickened primary walls remain capable of accommodating growth and bending rather than forming the heavily lignified reinforcement characteristic of sclerenchyma. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Primary wall; Uneven wall thickening
- **p013-i21** · diagram_label · classification · image · exam high
  - Sclerenchyma — names the right tissue drawing.
    - compare: What visually distinguishes sclerenchyma here? → The cells have thick walls surrounding relatively small internal spaces.
      - why: Why can a thick wall make the internal space look small? → Secondary wall material is deposited inward of the primary wall, reducing the available lumen. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Cell lumen; Secondary wall deposition
- **p013-i22** · diagram_label · structure · image · exam high
  - thin primary cell wall — points to a parenchyma cell wall.
    - what: What wall type is identified in parenchyma? → A thin primary cell wall.
      - how: How does a primary wall differ from a secondary wall? → The primary wall forms while a cell grows; a secondary wall is added inside it in certain differentiated cells, usually after expansion has largely ceased. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Plant cell growth; Cell wall
- **p013-i23** · diagram_label · structure · image · exam high
  - Irregularly Thickened Primary Cell Wall — points to the uneven wall thickening in collenchyma.
    - what: What is irregularly thickened in collenchyma? → The primary cell wall.
      - why: Why is the word 'primary' important? → It prevents confusing collenchyma thickening with the lignified secondary-wall formation characteristic of sclerenchyma. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Primary and secondary cell walls; Collenchyma
- **p013-i24** · diagram_label · structure · image · exam high
  - thick secondary cell wall — points to the thick inner wall layer of a sclerenchyma cell.
    - what: Which wall layer is conspicuously thick in sclerenchyma? → The secondary cell wall.
      - how: How does this layer contribute to support? → Its thickness and lignification strengthen the wall and increase resistance to deformation. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Secondary wall; Lignin; Mechanical strength
- **p013-i25** · diagram_label · structure · image · exam medium
  - primary cell wall — points to the outer primary wall of a sclerenchyma cell.
    - what: Does the sclerenchyma illustration also identify a primary wall? → Yes. It labels both the primary wall and the thick secondary wall.
      - how: How are these two wall layers related spatially? → The secondary wall is deposited on the inner side of the primary wall, toward the cell lumen. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Primary wall; Secondary wall; Cell lumen
- **p013-i26** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Features: 1. Cell shape | Parenchyma: Isodiametric cells which are oval, spherical or polygonal in shape | Collenchyma: Circular, oval or polyhedral | Sclerenchyma: Variable in shape. Fibres and sclereids
    - compare: How does the table compare cell shape across the three tissues? → Parenchyma is described as isodiametric with oval, spherical or polygonal cells; collenchyma as circular, oval or polyhedral; sclerenchyma as variable, including fibres and sclereids.
      - what: What does isodiametric mean? → The cell has approximately similar dimensions in different directions, rather than being strongly elongated along one axis. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How do fibres and sclereids explain sclerenchyma's variable shape? → Fibres are generally elongated, while sclereids can be shorter and irregular or differently shaped. Shape varies with cell type and location. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Three-dimensional cell shape; Tissue; Fibres and sclereids
- **p013-i27** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Features: 2. Cell wall | Parenchyma: Thin cellulosic cell wall | Collenchyma: Uneven thickening on their cell wall. | Sclerenchyma: Lignified secondary cell wall present
    - compare: What is the most useful cell-wall distinction among these tissues? → Parenchyma has a thin cellulosic wall, collenchyma has uneven wall thickening, and sclerenchyma has a lignified secondary wall.
      - why: Why does this distinguish two kinds of supporting tissue? → Collenchyma's thickened primary walls provide relatively flexible support; lignified sclerenchyma walls provide more rigid reinforcement. _(outside the book)_
      - what: What is lignin in this context? → Lignin is a complex wall-deposited substance that increases rigidity and contributes to the strength of lignified plant cells. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Cellulose; Primary and secondary walls; Lignin
- **p013-i28** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Features: 3. Cytoplasm | Parenchyma: Abundant | Collenchyma: Present | Sclerenchyma: Absent
    - compare: How does cytoplasm differ among the three tissues? → The table lists abundant cytoplasm in parenchyma, cytoplasm present in collenchyma and absent in sclerenchyma.
      - why: Why is cytoplasm absent in the mature sclerenchyma cells described here? → These cells typically lose their living protoplast during maturation, leaving the thick wall to perform a supporting role. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Cytoplasm; Living protoplast; Cell maturation
- **p013-i29** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Features: 4. Nucleus | Parenchyma: Present, (Living tissue) | Collenchyma: Present, (Living tissue) | Sclerenchyma: Absent (Dead tissue)
    - compare: Which tissues are classified as living and which as dead here? → Parenchyma and collenchyma are living with nuclei present; sclerenchyma is described as dead with the nucleus absent.
      - how: How can dead cells still form useful tissue? → A cell wall can persist after the protoplast is lost. In sclerenchyma, this durable wall provides mechanical support without requiring ongoing cellular metabolism. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Nucleus; Protoplast; Cell wall; Mature plant cells
- **p013-i30** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Features: 5. Vacuoles | Parenchyma: Large vacuole | Collenchyma: Vacuolated | Sclerenchyma: Absent
    - compare: How are vacuoles compared? → Parenchyma has a large vacuole, collenchyma is vacuolated, and sclerenchyma lacks vacuoles in the mature condition described.
      - how: How does a vacuole help a living plant cell? → It stores cell sap and helps generate turgor pressure as water enters the cell, contributing to cell firmness. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why is a sclerenchyma lumen not the same thing as a vacuole? → A vacuole is a membrane-bound compartment of a living protoplast. A lumen remaining after protoplast loss is simply the internal cavity bounded by the wall. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Vacuole; Cell sap; Turgor; Cell lumen
- **p013-i31** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Features: 6. Intercellular spaces | Parenchyma: Present | Collenchyma: Absent | Sclerenchyma: Absent
    - compare: Where does the table show intercellular spaces? → It lists spaces as present in parenchyma and absent in collenchyma and sclerenchyma.
      - how: How can spaces in parenchyma become functionally important? → They can permit gas movement; enlarged air spaces in aerenchyma also contribute to buoyancy in aquatic plants. _(outside the book)_
      - where: Where is an intercellular space relative to the wall? → It lies between neighbouring cells, not inside the lumen of a cell. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Cell boundaries; Intercellular space; Gas exchange
- **p013-i32** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Features: 7. Occurrence | Parenchyma: Basically packing tissue, All soft part of plant, Pith, cortex, medullary rays | Collenchyma: Dicot stems, petiole and beneath the epidermis. Absent in monocot and roots | Sclerenchyma: Dicot hypodermis, bundle sheath, pericycle, Seed, pulp of fruits.
    - compare: What occurrence lists does the page give? → Parenchyma is packing tissue in soft plant parts, including pith, cortex and medullary rays. Collenchyma occurs in dicot stems, petioles and beneath the epidermis, with absence in monocots and roots stated. Sclerenchyma is listed in dicot hypodermis, bundle sheath, pericycle, seed and fruit pulp.
      - where: What do the location terms mean? → The petiole is the leaf stalk; epidermis is the outer covering; hypodermis lies beneath it; cortex lies beneath the outer tissues; pith is a central ground-tissue region; medullary rays extend radially; a bundle sheath surrounds a vascular bundle; and the pericycle lies just inside the endodermis in a typical root. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why should these lists not be read as identical anatomy in every plant? → Tissue distribution varies with organ and plant group. A hypodermis may be collenchymatous or sclerenchymatous depending on the example, and sclereids in fruit pulp do not mean the entire pulp is sclerenchyma. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Dicot and monocot plants; Stem and root tissue regions; Petiole; Vascular bundle; Epidermis
- **p013-i33** · table_row · comparison · image · exam high
  - Features: 8. Functions | Parenchyma: Food storage, Photosynthesis | Collenchyma: Provide tensile strength, Mechanical support, Photosynthesis | Sclerenchyma: Protection from stress and strain, Mechanical strength
    - compare: How do the listed functions differ? → Parenchyma stores food and performs photosynthesis; collenchyma provides tensile strength, mechanical support and photosynthesis; sclerenchyma protects against stress and strain and supplies mechanical strength.
      - how: How should photosynthesis be understood for these tissue categories? → Photosynthesis requires chloroplasts. Chloroplast-bearing parenchyma is chlorenchyma, and collenchyma can photosynthesise where its living cells contain chloroplasts; tissue membership alone does not guarantee photosynthesis. _(outside the book)_
      - what: What does tensile strength mean? → It is resistance to being pulled apart. Stress refers to force per unit area, while strain describes relative deformation; these terms help explain how supporting tissues resist mechanical loads. _(outside the book)_
        - why: Why can support remain after sclerenchyma cells die? → Support depends chiefly on the persistent thickened wall rather than on active contraction or metabolism. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Food storage; Photosynthesis and chloroplasts; Mechanical support; Cell wall
- **p013-i34** · question · question · both · exam high
  - Q. All are parts of xylem except:
    - which: Which option is not a part of xylem? → A. Companion cells. They are associated with phloem. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How should xylem constituents be organised for this question? → Xylem includes tracheids, vessels, xylem parenchyma and xylem fibres. The printed 'Parenchyma' option refers to xylem parenchyma in this context. _(outside the book)_
      - compare: How does xylem differ functionally from phloem? → Xylem mainly conducts water and minerals and also supports the plant. Phloem transports organic food materials, with companion cells supporting sieve-tube function. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Vascular tissue; Xylem; Phloem; Transport in plants
- **p013-i35** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - A. Companion cells
    - which: Why is this the required exception? → Companion cells belong to phloem rather than xylem. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How do companion cells assist phloem transport? → They maintain close metabolic connections with sieve-tube elements and help support their transport activity. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Phloem; Sieve-tube elements
- **p013-i36** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - B. Tracheid
    - what: Is a tracheid a xylem constituent? → Yes. Tracheids are water-conducting and supporting xylem cells. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How do tracheids conduct water? → They are elongated cells, typically dead at functional maturity, with water passing between them through pits in their walls. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Xylem; Cell-wall pits; Water conduction
- **p013-i37** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - C. Parenchyma
    - what: Does parenchyma occur in xylem? → Yes. Xylem parenchyma is a constituent of xylem. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How can parenchyma be both a simple tissue and a component of a complex tissue? → Parenchyma describes a cell/tissue type. A complex tissue such as xylem combines different cell types, including parenchyma, into a functional system. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Simple and complex tissues; Parenchyma; Xylem
- **p013-i38** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - D. Xylem vessel
    - what: Is a xylem vessel a constituent of xylem? → Yes. It is a water-conducting structure. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How is a vessel formed? → Vessel elements join end to end, with perforated end walls permitting water movement along the resulting conduit. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Xylem; Vessel elements; Water transport
- **p013-i39** · question · question · both · exam high
  - Q. Lignin is present in cell wall of which tissue:
    - which: Which tissue is the correct answer? → b. Sclerenchyma. The comparison table specifies its lignified secondary cell wall.
      - why: Why is lignin associated with mechanical strength? → Its deposition reinforces the wall and increases rigidity, helping the tissue resist deformation. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Lignin; Cell wall; Sclerenchyma
- **p013-i40** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - a. Parenchyma
    - which: Is parenchyma the intended answer? → No. The table describes its wall as thin and cellulosic.
      - why: Why is cellulose not interchangeable with lignin? → They are different wall substances: cellulose forms structural microfibrils, whereas lignin is a reinforcing polymer deposited in lignified walls. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Cellulose; Lignin; Parenchyma wall
- **p013-i41** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - b. Sclerenchyma
    - which: Is this the correct option? → Yes. Sclerenchyma is listed as having a lignified secondary cell wall.
      - how: How does this wall relate to its function? → A thick lignified wall provides persistent mechanical reinforcement even after the cell loses its living contents. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Sclerenchyma; Secondary wall; Lignification
- **p013-i42** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - c. Collenchyma
    - which: Is collenchyma the intended lignified tissue? → No. The table instead emphasises its uneven primary-wall thickening.
      - why: Why might this distractor seem plausible? → Both collenchyma and sclerenchyma provide support, but their characteristic wall composition and mechanical properties differ. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Collenchyma and sclerenchyma; Wall thickening
- **p013-i43** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - d. None
    - which: Can 'None' be selected? → No. Sclerenchyma is a listed tissue with a lignified wall.
      - how: How can this option be eliminated directly from the page? → Read the sclerenchyma cell in the cell-wall comparison row, which explicitly says 'Lignified secondary cell wall present'.
  - prerequisites: Reading a comparison table; Sclerenchyma wall
- **p013-i44** · question · question · both · exam high
  - Q. Specialised cell of parenchyma present in aquatic plants to provide them buoyancy
    - which: Which option names the specialised parenchyma? → c. Aerenchyma. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How does aerenchyma contribute to buoyancy? → Its large air-filled intercellular spaces reduce the tissue's average density, helping aquatic plant parts remain supported in water. _(outside the book)_
      - what: Is aerenchyma a single specialised cell? → More precisely, it is a tissue arrangement of parenchyma with prominent air spaces. The question's 'specialised cell' wording is loose. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Parenchyma; Intercellular air spaces; Density; Buoyancy
- **p013-i45** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - a. Chlorenchyma
    - what: What is chlorenchyma? → It is chloroplast-containing parenchyma specialised for photosynthesis. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why is it not the intended buoyancy answer? → The defining feature tested is large air spaces, which identifies aerenchyma, rather than chloroplasts. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Chloroplast; Photosynthesis; Parenchyma
- **p013-i46** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - b. Sieve tube
    - what: What is a sieve tube? → It is a conducting structure of phloem formed by sieve-tube elements. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why is it unsuitable here? → Its principal role is transport of organic food materials, not forming air-rich parenchyma for buoyancy. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Phloem; Food transport
- **p013-i47** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - c. Aerenchyma
    - which: Is this the correct option? → Yes. Aerenchyma is the air-space-rich parenchyma associated with buoyancy in aquatic plants. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How do its spaces help besides contributing to buoyancy? → They provide internal pathways for gas movement, helping aerate submerged or waterlogged plant parts. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Intercellular spaces; Buoyancy; Gas movement
- **p013-i48** · answer_option · question · both · exam high
  - d. Companion cells
    - what: What tissue system contains companion cells? → Phloem, where they are closely associated with sieve-tube elements. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why do companion cells not answer the buoyancy question? → Their association is with sieve-tube metabolism and transport, not with large air-filled spaces characteristic of aerenchyma. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Phloem; Sieve-tube elements; Aerenchyma
- **p013-i49** · question · question · both · exam high
  - Q. Which statement is incorrect with reference to cardiac tissue:
    - which: Which numbered statement is incorrect? → Statement 2. Cardiac muscle is involuntary, not voluntary. _(outside the book)_
      - compare: How do the other two statements fit cardiac muscle? → Cardiac muscle is striated like skeletal muscle, but involuntary like visceral smooth muscle. This is the intended meaning of having features of both. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why must appearance and control be assessed separately? → Striations describe the organisation of contractile structures, whereas voluntary or involuntary describes control. A striped appearance does not establish conscious control. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Muscle tissue; Skeletal muscle; Visceral smooth muscle; Voluntary and involuntary control; Striations
- **p013-i50** · list_point · fact · both · exam high
  - 1. They are striated
    - what: Is the striation statement correct for cardiac tissue? → Yes. Cardiac muscle cells show striations. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why are striations visible? → Actin and myosin filaments are arranged in repeated contractile units called sarcomeres, producing an alternating banded appearance. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Muscle fibres; Actin and myosin; Sarcomere
- **p013-i51** · list_point · fact · both · exam high
  - 2. They are voluntary in nature
    - which: Is this statement correct? → No. Cardiac muscle contracts involuntarily. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How can the heart beat without conscious commands? → Specialised pacemaker cells initiate rhythmic electrical activity that spreads through the heart. Autonomic nerves and hormones modify its rate and force rather than requiring a conscious command for each beat. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Involuntary muscle; Electrical excitation; Pacemaker cells; Autonomic nervous system
- **p013-i52** · list_point · comparison · both · exam high
  - 3. They have features of both skeletal and visceral tissue.
    - compare: Which features of skeletal and visceral tissue are combined in cardiac tissue? → Cardiac tissue resembles skeletal muscle in being striated and visceral smooth muscle in being involuntary. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why does this not mean cardiac muscle is a mixture of the other two types? → It is a distinct muscle type sharing selected properties with them. The comparison concerns appearance and control, not an anatomical mixture. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Skeletal muscle; Visceral smooth muscle; Striations; Involuntary control

### Page 14 · passed after 0 round(s)

Four practice MCQs, including one continued from the previous page, revise cardiac muscle, epidermal protection, stomatal gas exchange and tendons. Standard textbook answers are c. 2 only for the cardiac question, b. Epidermis for the protective-layer question, b. Stomata for leaf gaseous exchange, and b. Tendon for muscle–bone attachment. The protective-layer question's claim that the epidermis is 'thickest' needs qualification.

- **p014-i1** · question · question · both · exam high
  - Q. Which statement is incorrect with reference to cardiac tissue:
1. They are striated
2. They are voluntary in nature
3. They have features of both skeletal and visceral tissue.
Option:
a. 1 only
b. 1 and 3.
c. 2 only.
d. All are correct
    - which: Which statement is incorrect, and which option should be selected? → Statement 2 is incorrect: cardiac muscle is involuntary, not voluntary. The intended answer is c. 2 only. _(outside the book)_
    - compare: In what sense does cardiac tissue share features with skeletal and visceral muscle? → Cardiac muscle is striated like skeletal muscle, but its contraction is involuntary like visceral smooth muscle. This limited comparison explains statement 3; it does not mean these tissues have identical structures. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why do striations not imply voluntary control? → Striations reflect the regular arrangement of contractile proteins, whereas voluntary control concerns how contraction is initiated and regulated. Cardiac muscle has organised contractile units but does not require a conscious command for each heartbeat. _(outside the book)_
        - how: How can the heart contract without a conscious command? → Specialised cardiac pacemaker cells generate rhythmic electrical activity that spreads through the heart. Autonomic nerves modify this activity rather than requiring a voluntary command for each contraction. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Muscle contraction; Striated and unstriated muscle; Voluntary and involuntary control
- **p014-i2** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - a. 1 only
    - what: What would choosing this option claim? → It would identify statement 1, that cardiac tissue is striated, as the sole incorrect statement.
      - why: Why should this option be rejected? → Cardiac muscle does have striations. Calling it involuntary does not remove its striated appearance. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Meaning of striations; Incorrect-statement MCQ format
- **p014-i3** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - b. 1 and 3.
    - what: Which statements does this option identify as incorrect? → It identifies statements 1 and 3: striations and the sharing of features with skeletal and visceral tissue.
      - why: Why is this not the intended answer? → Cardiac muscle is striated, and the comparison in statement 3 is valid when it refers to skeletal-like striations and visceral-like involuntary control. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Cardiac, skeletal and smooth muscle; Voluntary and involuntary control
- **p014-i4** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - c. 2 only.
    - which: Is this the intended correct option? → Yes. Statement 2 alone is incorrect because cardiac tissue is involuntary. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why is cardiac muscle classified as involuntary? → Its rhythmic contractions occur without conscious direction, although their rate and force can be modified by autonomic and hormonal influences. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Meaning of involuntary contraction
- **p014-i5** · answer_option · fact · both · exam medium
  - d. All are correct
    - what: What does this option assert? → It asserts that all three statements about cardiac tissue are correct.
      - why: Why must it be rejected? → The statement describing cardiac tissue as voluntary is false, so the three statements cannot all be correct. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Voluntary and involuntary muscle
- **p014-i6** · question · question · both · exam high
  - Q. …………… layer is thickest and helps in protection against water loss and further secrete a waxy substance on outer surface:
    - which: Which option best matches the protective-layer and waxy-secretion clues? → The intended answer is b. Epidermis. Epidermal cells of exposed aerial plant parts commonly produce a waxy cuticle that helps reduce water loss. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How does a waxy outer covering reduce water loss? → The cuticle provides a water-resistant barrier over the epidermis, reducing evaporation through the exposed surface. _(outside the book)_
    - exception: Is epidermis universally the thickest plant layer? → No. Epidermis is commonly a single cell layer. Some plants in dry habitats have a thicker epidermis or a thick cuticle, but the question gives no condition that makes 'thickest' a general rule. _(outside the book)_
      - difference: How should epidermis, cuticle and suberin be distinguished? → Epidermis is a cellular covering tissue; the cuticle is a waxy, cutin-containing covering deposited on its outer surface; suberin is a water-resistant wall material associated especially with cork cells. They are not interchangeable names. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Plant organs and their outer surfaces; Evaporation and water loss; Difference between a tissue and a deposited substance
- **p014-i7** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - a. Sclerenchyma
    - what: What is sclerenchyma? → It is a supporting plant tissue whose cells generally have thick, lignified walls and are dead at maturity. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why does its thick wall not make it the answer? → The question's decisive clue is an outer protective layer producing a waxy covering. Sclerenchyma primarily provides mechanical strength; thick cell walls are not the same as an epidermal covering. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Plant cell wall; Mechanical support in plants
- **p014-i8** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - b. Epidermis
    - what: What is the epidermis in a plant? → It is the outer covering tissue of young plant organs, separating underlying tissues from the surroundings. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How does it fit the waxy-substance clue? → In exposed aerial parts, epidermal cells commonly deposit a waxy cuticle on their outer surface, helping limit water loss. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Plant tissue; Cuticle
- **p014-i9** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - c. Parenchyma
    - what: What is parenchyma? → Parenchyma is a simple permanent plant tissue composed of living cells, commonly with relatively thin walls, performing functions such as storage and, in specialised forms, photosynthesis. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why is it not the intended protective-layer answer? → The question identifies a surface covering that produces a waxy barrier. That role points to epidermis, rather than general storage or ground-tissue parenchyma. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Living plant cells; Simple permanent tissues
- **p014-i10** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - d. Suberin
    - what: What is suberin? → Suberin is a water-resistant substance deposited in certain plant cell walls, notably those of cork cells. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why is suberin a plausible distractor but not the intended answer? → It is associated with restricting water movement, but it is a wall substance rather than the epidermal tissue layer that produces an outer cuticle. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Cork cells; Difference between tissue and cell-wall material
- **p014-i11** · question · question · both · exam high
  - Q. Site of gaseous exchange on leaf is:
    - which: Which option identifies the usual site of gaseous exchange on a leaf? → b. Stomata. These pores connect the leaf's internal air spaces with the surrounding atmosphere. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How do gases pass through stomata? → Gases diffuse through open stomatal pores along concentration gradients. Carbon dioxide can enter for photosynthesis, while oxygen and water vapour can move out under the relevant gradients. _(outside the book)_
      - difference: How does the exchange site differ from its regulating cells? → The stomatal pore is the passage for gas movement; guard cells bordering it regulate the size of that passage. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Diffusion; Leaf epidermis; Photosynthesis and respiration
- **p014-i12** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - a. Guard cells
    - what: What are guard cells? → They are specialised epidermal cells bordering a stomatal pore and regulating its opening. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How do guard cells regulate gaseous exchange? → Changes in their water content and turgor change their shape and the pore opening. They regulate the route of exchange rather than being the pore itself. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Cell turgor; Stomatal pore
- **p014-i13** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - b. Stomata
    - what: What are stomata? → Stomata are pores in the epidermis of leaves and some other aerial plant parts, associated with guard cells. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why are they suited to gaseous exchange? → An open pore provides a diffusion pathway between the external air and internal leaf air spaces, while guard cells allow its opening to be regulated. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Epidermis; Diffusion
- **p014-i14** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - c. Aerenchyma
    - what: What is aerenchyma? → It is parenchymatous tissue with large air spaces, common in aquatic plants, that helps internal gas movement and can contribute to buoyancy. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why does the presence of air spaces not make it the answer here? → Aerenchyma is an internal tissue adaptation. The question asks for the leaf's gas-exchange site with the atmosphere, for which stomata are the intended answer. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Parenchyma; Intercellular air spaces
- **p014-i15** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - d. Chlorenchyma
    - what: What is chlorenchyma? → It is parenchyma containing chloroplasts and specialised for photosynthesis. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why is a photosynthetic tissue not the answer to this question? → Photosynthetic cells use carbon dioxide and produce oxygen, but the question asks for the surface route through which gases enter and leave the leaf, not the tissue carrying out photosynthesis. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Parenchyma; Chloroplasts; Photosynthesis
- **p014-i16** · question · question · both · exam high
  - Q. Muscle is connected to bone via:
    - which: Which structure connects muscle to bone? → b. Tendon is the standard answer for the attachment of skeletal muscle to bone. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How does this connection help produce movement? → When skeletal muscle contracts, it develops tension that the tendon transmits to the attached bone. This pull can move the bone at a joint. _(outside the book)_
    - compare: How do tendon, ligament and cartilage differ in this context? → A tendon connects muscle to bone and transmits pulling force; a ligament typically connects bone to bone and stabilises a joint; cartilage provides support and, at many joint surfaces, a smooth covering rather than the muscle–bone attachment. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Skeletal muscle contraction; Bones and joints; Connective tissue
- **p014-i17** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - a. Ligament
    - what: What does a ligament typically connect? → A ligament typically connects one bone to another at a joint. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why is ligament incorrect for muscle–bone attachment? → Its usual role is joint stabilisation between bones, whereas a tendon provides the muscle's attachment and transmits its pull to bone. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Joint; Bone-to-bone connection
- **p014-i18** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - b. Tendon
    - what: What is a tendon? → A tendon is a strong fibrous connective-tissue structure that commonly attaches skeletal muscle to bone. _(outside the book)_
      - how: How is its structure suited to transmitting muscle force? → Its bundles of collagen fibres provide high tensile strength, allowing it to withstand and transmit the pulling force generated by muscle contraction. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Connective tissue; Collagen fibres; Pulling force
- **p014-i19** · answer_option · fact · both · exam high
  - c. Cartilage
    - what: What role can cartilage perform at a joint? → Articular cartilage covers the ends of bones at many joints, providing a smooth surface and helping distribute loads. _(outside the book)_
      - why: Why is cartilage not the correct attachment structure? → Joint-surface support and reduction of friction differ from connecting a muscle to a bone and transmitting the muscle's pull. The latter is the role of a tendon. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Connective tissue; Joint surfaces
- **p014-i20** · answer_option · fact · both · exam low
  - d. None
    - what: What would choosing 'None' imply? → It would mean that none of ligament, tendon or cartilage answers the muscle–bone attachment question.
      - why: Why should this option be rejected? → Tendon is already listed and is the standard structure connecting skeletal muscle to bone. _(outside the book)_
  - prerequisites: Tendon as muscle–bone attachment
